Reeder was peculiarly good, and though he had not as yet met the housekeeper, he was quite certain that the rather beautiful face he had glimpsed for a moment did not belong to any sad woman who had seen a lot of trouble. As he dressed leisurely for dinner, he wondered why Miss Olga Crewe had been so anxious that she should not be seen coming from the proprietor’s suite. A natural and proper modesty, no doubt; and modesty was the quality in woman of which Mr. Reeder most heartily approved.

He was struggling with his tie when Daver, who seemed to have constituted himself a sort of personal attendant, knocked at the door and asked permission to come in. He was a little breathless and carried a number of press cuttings in his hand.

“You were talking about two gentlemen, Mr. Willington and Mr. Holden,” he said. “The names seemed rather familiar. I had the irritating sense of knowing them without knowing them, if you understand, dear Mr. Reeder? And then I recalled the circumstances.” He flourished the press cuttings. “I saw their names here.”

Mr. Reeder, staring at his reflection in the glass, adjusted his tie nicely.

“Here?” he repeated mechanically, and looking round, accepted the printed slips which his host thrust upon him.

“I am, as you probably know, Mr. Reeder, a humble disciple of Lombroso and of those other great criminologists who have elevated the study of abnormality to a science. It was Miss Belman who quite unconsciously directed my thoughts to the Flack organization, and during the past day or two I have been getting a number of particulars concerning those miscreants. The names of Holden and Willington occur. They were two detectives who went out in search of Flack and never returned. I remember their disappearance very well, now the matter is recalled to my mind. There was also a third gentleman who disappeared.”

Mr. Reeder nodded.

“Ah, you remember?” said Mr. Daver triumphantly. “Naturally you would. A lawyer named Biggerthorpe, who was called from his office one day on some excuse, and was never seen again. May I add”﻿—he smiled good-humouredly﻿—“that Mr. Biggerthorpe has never stayed here? Why should you imagine he had, Mr. Reeder?”

===Chinese===
正说话间，走廊尽头的一扇门开了开，旋即合上。里德先生从不放过任何东西，门关死前瞥见了一条人影。

“那是谁的房间？”他问。

这回达弗先生是真的窘了。

“那间，”他带着一声不安的小咳，“是我的套间。您方才看见的是伯顿太太（Mrs. Burton），我的管家——一个安静的、颇为忧伤的人，一辈子受过许多磨难。”

“人生嘛，”里德先生说得老生常谈，“总是充满磨难。”达弗先生悲哀地摇着头表示同意。

J. G. 里德的目力格外出众。他虽还没见过这位管家，却十拿九稳：他霎时瞥见的那张相当俏丽的脸，绝不属于哪个饱经忧患的悲伤女人。他一边慢条斯理地换晚装，一边纳闷：奥尔加·克鲁小姐为什么那样生怕被人瞧见她从店主的套间里出来？想必是一种自然而得体的娇羞；而娇羞，正是女人的品质中最令里德先生由衷赞许的一种。

他正跟领带较劲，达弗敲了敲门，请求进来——此人俨然自封了随身侍应的角色。他微微气喘，手里拿着一沓剪报。

“您方才说起两位先生，威林顿先生和霍尔登先生，”他说，“这两个名字听着怪耳熟。我有种叫人心痒的感觉：认识他们，又不认识他们——您明白吗，亲爱的里德先生？后来我把当时的情形想起来了。”他把剪报一挥，“我在这里面看到了他们的名字。”

里德先生盯着镜中的自己，把领带细细理妥。

“这里面？”他机械地重复了一句，回过头来，接过主人塞给他的那几张印刷纸条。

“您大概已经知道，里德先生，我是龙勃罗梭以及另几位将变态研究升华为科学的伟大犯罪学家的卑微信徒。正是贝尔曼小姐，全然无意地把我的思路引向了弗拉克那个团伙；这一两天，我一直搜集有关那帮恶徒的种种详情。霍尔登和威林顿这两个名字就在其中。他们是两名出去搜捕弗拉克、从此一去不返的侦探。此事一经提起，我对他们的失踪记得清清楚楚。此外还有第三位先生失踪了。”

里德先生点头。

“啊，您记得吧？”达弗先生得意洋洋地说，“您自然记得。一位名叫比格索普（Biggerthorpe）的律师，某日被人寻个由头从事务所叫出去，从此再没人见过。容我补一句”——他和气地笑笑——“比格索普先生从未在本店住过。您怎么会联想到他住过呢，里德先生？”

===Original===
“I never did.” Mr. Reeder gave blandness for blandness. “Biggerthorpe? I had forgotten him. He would have been an important witness against Flack if he’d ever been caught﻿—hum!”

And then:

“You are a student of criminal practices, Mr. Daver?”

“A humble one,” said Mr. Daver, and his humility was manifest in his attitude.

And then he suddenly dropped his voice to a hoarse whisper.

“Shall I tell you something, Mr. Reeder?”

“You may tell me,” said Mr. Reeder, as he buttoned his waistcoat, “anything that pleases you. I am in the mood for stories. In this delightful atmosphere, amidst these beautiful surroundings, I should prefer﻿—um﻿—fairy stories﻿—or shall we say ghost stories? Is Larmes Keep haunted, Mr. Daver? Ghosts are my specialty. I have probably seen and arrested more ghosts than any other living representative of the law. Sometime I intend writing a monumental work on the subject. *Ghosts I Have Seen, or a Guide to the Spirit World*, in sixty-three volumes. You were about to say﻿—?”

===Chinese===
“我从来没这么想。”里德先生以淡然还其淡然，“比格索普？我把他忘了。弗拉克当初要是落了网，他本会是重要的证人——哼！”

接着又说：

“您是犯罪手法的研习者吧，达弗先生？”

“一个卑微的研习者。”达弗先生说，那份谦卑在他的姿态里显豁无遗。

随后他忽然把声音压成沙哑的耳语。

“我告诉您一件事好吗，里德先生？”

“您只管讲，”里德先生一边扣着马甲的扣子一边说，“讲什么都随您高兴。我正有听故事的心情。在这样愉快的气氛里，置身这样优美的环境中，我宁可听——嗯——童话故事——或者咱们不妨说，鬼故事？拉尔姆宅闹鬼吗，达弗先生？鬼魂正是我的专行。我平生见过、也逮捕过的鬼魂，多半比任何一位在世执法人士都多。改日我打算就这个题目写一部煌煌巨著：*《我见过的鬼，或曰灵界指南》*，凡六十三卷。您方才要说的是——？”

===Original===
“I was about to say,” said Mr. Daver, and his voice was curiously strained, “that in my opinion Flack himself once stayed here. I have not mentioned this fact to Miss Belman, but I am convinced in my mind that I am not in error. Seven years ago”﻿—he was very impressive﻿—“a gray-bearded, rather thin-faced man came here at ten o’clock at night and asked for a lodging. He had plenty of money, but this did not influence me. Ordinarily I should have asked him to make the usual application, but it was late, a bitterly cold and snowy night, and I hadn’t the heart to turn one of his age away from my door.”

“How long did he stay?” asked Mr. Reeder. “And why do you think he was Flack?”

“Because”﻿—Daver’s voice had sunk until it was an eerie moan﻿—“he left just as Ravini left﻿—early one morning, without paying his bill, and left his pajamas behind him!”

Very slowly Mr. Reeder turned his head and surveyed the host.

“That comes into the category of humorous stories, and I am too hungry to laugh,” he said calmly. “What time do we dine?”

The gong sounded at that moment.

Margaret Belman usually dined at a table apart from the other guests. She went red and felt more than a little awkward when Mr. Reeder came across to her table, dragging a chair with him, and ordered another place to be set. The other three guests dined at separate tables.

“An unsociable lot of people,” said Mr. Reeder as he shook out his napkin and glanced round the room.

“What do you think of Mr. Daver?”

J. G. Reeder smiled gently.

“He is a very amusing person,” he said, and she laughed, but grew serious immediately.

“Have you found out anything about Ravini?”

Mr. Reeder shook his head.

“I had a talk with the hall porter; he seems a very honest and straightforward fellow. He told me that when he came down the morning after Ravini disappeared, the front door had been unbolted and unlocked. An observant fellow. Who is Mrs. Burton?” he asked abruptly.

===Chinese===
“我方才要说的是，”达弗先生说，声音出奇地紧绷，“依我看，弗拉克本人就在这儿住过。这件事我没有对贝尔曼小姐提过，但我心里坚信自己没有弄错。七年前的”——他语气极有分量——“一个留着灰白胡须、脸相颇瘦的汉子，夜里十点钟跑到这儿来投宿。他有的是钱，但这没能左右我。按说，我本该让他照章递申请，可天太晚了，又是个滴水成冰的雪夜，我不忍心把这样一把年纪的人从我门口撵走。”

“他住了多久？”里德先生问，“您又凭什么认为他是弗拉克？”

“因为”——达弗的声音低下去，低成一声鬼气森森的呜咽——“他的走法，跟拉维尼如出一辙——一天清早，不结账，还把睡衣睡裤留在了身后！”

里德先生极慢地转过头，端详着这位主人。

“这属于滑稽故事一类，我饿得笑不动了。”他平静地说，“我们几点开饭？”

恰在此时，锣响了。

玛格丽特·贝尔曼平日总是独自坐在一张桌旁用餐，不与其他客人同桌。这回里德先生拖了把椅子朝她的桌子走过来，吩咐再添一副餐具，她红了脸，觉得颇为尴尬。另外三位客人各自坐在单独的桌上。

“一帮不爱交际的人。”里德先生抖开餐巾，环视餐厅，说道。

“您觉得达弗先生这个人怎么样？”

J. G. 里德淡淡一笑。

“他是个非常有趣的人。”他说。她笑了，随即又严肃起来。

“拉维尼的事，您查出什么了吗？”

里德先生摇头。

“我跟门房聊了聊；他看起来是个非常诚实直爽的人。他告诉我，拉维尼失踪的第二天早晨他下楼时，前门的插销和锁具都开着。是个眼力不错的人。伯顿太太是谁？”他唐突地问。

===Original===
“The housekeeper.” Margaret smiled and shook her head. “She is rather a miserable lady who spends quite a lot of time hinting at the good times she should be having instead of being ‘buried alive’﻿—those are her words﻿—at Siltbury.”

Mr. Reeder put down his knife and fork.

“Dear me!” he said mildly. “Is she a lady who has seen better days?”

Margaret laughed softly.

“I should have thought she had never had such a time as she is having now,” she said. “She’s rather common and terribly illiterate. Her accounts that come up to me are fearful and wonderful things! But, seriously, I think she must have been in good circumstances. The first night I was here I went into her room to ask about an account. I did not understand﻿—of course it was a waste of time, for books are mysteries to her﻿—and she was sitting at a table admiring her hands.”

“Hands?” he said.

She nodded.

“They were covered with the most beautiful rings you could possibly imagine,” said Margaret, and was satisfied with the impression she made, for Mr. Reeder dropped knife and fork to his plate with a crash.

“Rings﻿—?”

===Chinese===
“管家呗。”玛格丽特笑着摇摇头，“她是位挺苦哈哈的太太，把大把工夫都花在暗示上：说自己本该享着福，却在这儿‘活埋’在西尔特伯里——这是她的原话。”

里德先生放下了刀叉。

“哎呀！”他温和地说，“她是位见过好日子的太太？”

玛格丽特轻轻笑了。

“照我看，她这辈子就没有过现在这样的好日子，”她说，“她挺俗气，又目不识丁得厉害。交到我手里的那些账目，真是又骇人又离奇！不过说正经的，我想她从前家境一定不错。我来的头一晚，为一份账目进了她的房间。我没弄明白——当然，那是白费工夫，账本对她来说是天书——她正坐在桌前，欣赏自己的那双手。”

“手？”他说。

她点头。

“那双手上戴满了你所能想象的最漂亮的戒指。”玛格丽特说，并且对自己造成的效果十分满意——里德先生咔哒一声，把刀叉摔回了盘子。

“戒指——？”

===Original===
“Huge diamonds and emeralds. They took my breath away. The moment she saw me, she put her hands behind her, and the next morning she explained that they were presents given to her by a theatrical lady who had stayed here and that they had no value.”

“Props, in fact,” said Mr. Reeder.

“What is a prop?” she asked curiously, and Mr. Reeder waggled his head, and she had learnt that when he waggled his head in that fashion he was advertising his high spirits and good-humour.

After dinner he sent a waitress to find Mr. Daver, and when that gentleman arrived, Mr. Reeder had to tell him that he had a lot of work to do and request the loan of blotting pad and a special writing table for his room. Margaret wondered why he had not asked her, but she supposed that it was because he did not know that such things came into her province.

“You’re a great writer, Mr. Reeder﻿—he-he!” Daver was convulsed at his own little joke. “So am I! I am never happy without a pen in my hand. Tell me, as a matter of interest, do you do your best work in the morning or in the evening? Personally, it is a question that I have never decided to my own satisfaction.”

“I shall now write steadily till two o’clock,” said Mr. Reeder, glancing at his watch. “That is a habit of years. From nine to two are my writing hours, after which I smoke a cigarette, drink a glass of milk﻿—would you be good enough to see that I have a glass of milk put in my room at once?﻿—and from two I sleep steadily till nine.”

Margaret Belman was an interested and somewhat startled audience of this personal confession. It was unusual in Mr. Reeder to speak of himself, unthinkable that he should discuss his work. In all her life she had not met an individual who was more reticent about his private affairs. Perhaps the holiday spirit was on him, she thought. He was certainly younger-looking that evening than she had ever known him.

She went out to find Mrs. Burton and convey the wishes of the guest. The woman accepted the order with a sniff.

===Chinese===
“硕大的钻石和祖母绿。看得我气都透不过来。她一看见我，就把手藏到背后；第二天早上她解释说，那是一位在这儿住过的戏剧界女士送她的礼物，根本不值钱。”

“说白了，是道具。”里德先生说。

“道具是什么意思？”她好奇地问。里德先生晃了晃脑袋；她已经摸清了，每逢他这样晃脑袋，就是在昭告他自己兴致高昂、心情大好。

晚饭后他差一名女侍去找达弗先生；那位先生一到，里德先生便不得不告诉他，自己有许多工作要做，并请求借用一叠吸墨纸垫和一张特制的写字台放到他房里。玛格丽特纳闷他为什么不找她，可她猜想，那是因为他不知道这些事归她管。

“您是位大作家，里德先生——嘿嘿！”达弗被自己的小笑话逗得直不起腰。“我也是！手里不握支笔，我就从没快活过。说说看，纯粹出于兴趣——您的妙文是上午写得好，还是晚上写得好？就我个人而言，这个问题我从来没让自己满意地决定过。”

“我现在要埋头写到两点，”里德先生说着看了一眼表，“这是多年的习惯。从九点到两点是我的写作时间，写完我抽支烟，喝杯牛奶——可否劳驾吩咐马上在我房里放一杯牛奶？——从两点起我一觉稳睡到九点。”

这番自陈其私，玛格丽特·贝尔曼听得津津有味，又有些吃惊。里德先生谈自己，本属罕事；谈论他的工作，更是不可想象。她生平还没见过一个对自己的私事讳莫如深到这个地步的人。兴许是假日的兴致上了他的身，她想。他那晚的气色，确实比她历来所见的都要年轻。

她出去找伯顿太太，转达客人的愿望。那女人嗤了一声，把这吩咐接了下来。

===Original===
“Milk? He looks the kind of person who drinks milk. He’s nothing to be afraid of!”

“Why should he be afraid?” asked Margaret sharply, but the reproach was lost upon Mrs. Burton.

“Nobody likes detectives nosing about a place﻿—do they, Miss Belman? And he’s not my idea of a detective.”

“Who told you he was a detective?”

Mrs. Burton looked at her for a second from under her heavy lids, and then jerked her head in the direction of Daver’s office.

“He did,” she said. “Detectives! And me sitting here, slaving from morning till night, when I might be doing the grand in Paris or one of them places, with servants to wait on me instead of me waiting on people. It’s sickening!”

Twice since she had been at Larmes Keep, Margaret had witnessed these little outbursts of fretfulness and irritation. She had an idea that the faded woman would like some excuse to make her a confidante, but the excuse was neither found nor sought. Margaret had nothing in common with this rather dull and terribly ordinary lady, and they could find no mutual interest which would lead to the breakdown of the barriers. Mrs. Burton was a weakling; tears were never far from her eyes or voice, nor the sense of her mysterious grievances against the world far from her mind.

“They treat me like dirt,” she went on, her voice trembling with her feeble anger, “and she treats me worst of all. I asked her to come and have a cup of tea and a chat in my room the other day, and what do you think she said?”

“Whom are you talking about?” asked Margaret curiously. It did not occur to her that the “she” in question might be Olga Crewe. It would have required a very powerful effort of imagination to picture the cold and worldly Olga talking commonplaces with Mrs. Burton over a friendly cup of tea; yet it was of Olga that the woman spoke. But at the very suggestion that she was being questioned, her thin lips closed tight.

“Nobody in particular.﻿—Milk, did you say? I’ll take it up to him myself.”

Mr. Reeder was struggling into a dressing jacket when she brought the milk to him. One of the servants had already placed pen, ink, and stationery on the table, and there were two fat manuscript books visible to any caller, and anticipating eloquently Mr. Reeder’s literary activities.

He took the tray from the woman’s hand and put it on the table.

“You have a nice house, Mrs. Burton,” he said encouragingly. “A beautiful house. Have you been here long?”

“A few years,” she answered.

===Chinese===
“牛奶？他看着就是喝牛奶的那号人。没什么好怕的！”

“他有什么可怕的呢？”玛格丽特尖锐地问，可这番抢白对伯顿太太全然白费。

“谁也不喜欢侦探在地方上到处嗅探——是不是，贝尔曼小姐？再说，他也够不上我心目中的侦探。”

“谁告诉您他是侦探的？”

伯顿太太从厚眼皮底下看了她一秒钟，随即朝达弗办公室的方向猛一扬头。

“他说的，”她说，“侦探们！我呢，坐在这儿从早苦到晚，本来我可以在巴黎或随便哪个那种地方摆阔气，让仆人伺候我，而不是我伺候人。真叫人恶心！”

自打来到拉尔姆宅，这样的焦躁怨怼小发作，玛格丽特已见过两回。她有种感觉：这个憔悴的女人巴不得有个由头来把她当成知己，只是这由头既没人找，也没人给。玛格丽特与这位相当乏味、平凡得可怕的女人毫无共同语言，两人也寻不出什么共同兴趣来打破这层隔膜。伯顿太太是个软弱的人：眼泪从不曾离她的眼睛和嗓音太远，而那股对世界的莫名委屈感，也从不曾离她的心头太远。

“他们拿我当尘土作践，”她接着说，声音因微弱的怒气而发颤，“而她作践我作践得最凶。前几天我请她来我房间喝杯茶、聊聊天，你猜她怎么说？”

“您说的是谁呀？”玛格丽特好奇地问。她压根没想到那个“她”可能是奥尔加·克鲁。要想象冷若冰霜、满身俗气的奥尔加，肯坐下来陪着伯顿太太喝杯茶拉些家常闲话，得费天大的想象力；可这女人说的偏偏就是奥尔加。但一察觉自己正被盘问，她那两片薄嘴唇立刻闭得紧紧的。

“没什么特定的人。——牛奶，您是说？我自己给他端上去。”

她把牛奶送去时，里德先生正费劲地往身上套一件居家便服短褂。一个仆役早已把笔、墨水和信纸摆上了桌，还有两册厚厚的稿本摆得让任何来客都看得见，雄辩地预示着里德先生的文学生涯。

他从女人手里接过托盘，放到桌上。

“您这宅子真好，伯顿太太，”他鼓励地说，“一座漂亮的宅子。您在这儿待得久吗？”

“有几年了。”她答道。

===Original===
She made as if to go, but lingered at the door. Mr. Reeder recognized the symptoms. Discreet she might be, a gossip she undoubtedly was, aching for human converse with any who could advance a programme of those trivialities which made up her conversational life.

“No, sir, we never get many visitors here. Mr. Daver likes to pick and choose.”

“And very wise of Mr. Daver. By the way, which is his room?”

She walked through the doorway and pointed along the corridor.

“Oh, yes, I remember, he told me. A charming situation. I saw you coming out this evening.”

“You have made a mistake﻿—I never go into his room,” said the woman sharply. “You may have seen﻿—” She stopped, and added﻿—“somebody else. Are you going to work late, sir?”

Mr. Reeder repeated in detail his plans for the evening.

“I should be glad if you would tell Mr. Daver that I do not wish to be interrupted. I am a very slow thinker, and the slightest disturbance to my train of thought is fatal to my﻿—er﻿—power of composition,” he said, as he closed the door upon her, and, waiting until she had time to get down the stairs, locked it and pushed home the one bolt.

===Chinese===
她作势要走，却在门边磨蹭。里德先生看出了这些症候。她也许谨慎，但无疑是个长舌妇，渴望跟任何一个能把她的谈资节目单往前推进的人聊上几句——那些鸡毛蒜皮，就是她谈话生活的全部内容。

“不，先生，我们这儿客人从来不多。达弗先生就爱挑挑拣拣。”

“达弗先生很有见地。顺便问一句，他的房间是哪间？”

她走到门口，朝走廊那头一指。

“哦，对了，我想起来了，他跟我说过。位置真好。今天傍晚我看见您从里面出来。”

“您弄错了——我从不进他的房间，”那女人尖刻地说，“您看见的可能是——”她顿住，又补一句——“别的什么人。您要熬夜工作吗，先生？”

里德先生把晚间的打算原原本本又说了一遍。

“劳驾您告诉达弗先生，我不愿被人打扰。我这个人思考很慢，思路受到一丝一毫的惊扰，对我的——呃——写作能力都是致命的。”他一边说，一边把她关在门外；等她来得及走下楼梯，他便锁上门，把那根门闩推到了底。

===Original===
He drew the heavy curtains across the open windows, pushed the writing table against the curtains so that they could not blow back, and, opening the two exercise books, so placed them that they formed a shade that prevented the light from falling upon the bed. This done, he changed quickly into a lounge suit, and, lying on the bed, pulled the coverlet over him and was asleep in five minutes.

Margaret Belman had it in her mind to send up to his room after eleven, before she herself retired, to discover whether there was anything he wanted, but fortunately she changed her mind﻿—fortunately, because Mr. Reeder had planned to snatch five solid hours’ sleep before he began his unofficial inspection of the house, or alternatively before the period arrived when it would be necessary that he should be wide-awake.

---

===Chinese===
他拉上厚窗帘，遮住敞开的窗，又把写字台抵住窗帘，好教它们吹不回来；然后翻开那两册练习簿，摆成一个遮罩，挡住灯光，不让它落到床上。做完这些，他迅速换上一套便装西服，往床上一躺，拉过床罩盖在身上，五分钟内便睡着了。

玛格丽特·贝尔曼本来打算十一点以后、自己歇下之前，打发人上他房间去看看还缺什么，幸好她改了主意——之所以说幸好，是因为里德先生已经盘算好，先足足抓上五个钟头的觉，再开始他对这宅子的非官方巡查；或者说，再等那个需要他全然清醒的时刻到来。

---

===Original===
At two o’clock to the second he woke and sat up on the edge of the bed, blinking at the light. Opening one of his trunks, he took out a small wooden box from which he drew a spirit stove and the paraphernalia of tea-making. He lit the little lamp, and while the tiny tin kettle was boiling, he went to the bathroom, undressed, and lowered his shivering body into a cold bath. He returned fully dressed to find the kettle boiling.

Mr. Reeder was a very methodical man; he was, moreover, a careful man. All his life he had had a suspicion of milk. He used to wander round the suburban streets in the early hours of the morning, watch the cans hanging on the knockers, the bottles deposited in corners of doorsteps, and ruminate upon the enormous possibilities for wholesale murder that this lighthearted custom of milk delivery presented to the criminally minded. He had calculated that a nimble homicide, working on systematic lines, could decimate London in a month.

He drank his tea without milk, munched a biscuit, and then, methodically clearing away the spirit stove and kettle, he took from his grip a pair of thick-soled felt slippers and drew them on his feet. In his trunk he found a short length of stiff rubber, which, in the hands of a skillful man, was as deadly a weapon as a knife. This he put in the inside pocket of his jacket. He put his hand into the trunk again and brought out something that looked like a thin rubber sponge bag, except that it was fitted with two squares of mica and a small metal nozzle. He hesitated about this, turning it over and over in his hand, and eventually this went back into the trunk. The stubby Browning pistol, which was his next find, Mr. Reeder regarded with disfavour, for the value of firearms, except in the most desperate circumstances, had always seemed to him to be problematical.

The last thing to be extracted was a hollow bamboo, which contained another, and was in truth the fishing rod for which he had once expressed a desire. At the end of the thinner one was a spring loop, and after he had screwed the two lengths together, he fitted upon this loop a small electric hand lamp and carefully threaded the thin wires through the eyelets of the rod, connecting them up with a tiny switch at the handle, near where the average fisherman has his grip. He tested the switch, found it satisfactory, and when this was done he gave a final look round the room before extinguishing the table lamp.

In the broad light of day he would have presented a somewhat comic figure, sitting cross-legged on his bed, his long fishing rod reaching out to the middle of the room and resting on the footboard; but at the moment Mr. J. G. Reeder had no sense of the ridiculous, and, moreover, there were no witnesses.

From time to time he swayed the rod left and right, like an angler making a fresh cast. He was very wide-awake, his ears tuned to differentiate between the normal noises of the night﻿—the rustle of trees, the soft purr of the wind﻿—and the sounds which could only come from human activity.

He sat for more than half an hour, his fishing rod moving to and fro, and then he was suddenly conscious of a cold draught blowing from the door. He had heard no sound﻿—not so much as the clink of a lock; but he knew that the door was wide open.

Noiselessly he drew in the rod till it was clear of the posts of the bed, brought it round toward the door, paying out until it was a couple of yards from where he sat﻿—with one foot on the floor now, ready to leap or drop, as events dictated.

The end of the rod met with no obstruction. Reeder held his breath﻿—listening. The corridor outside was heavily carpeted. He expected no sound of footsteps. But people must breathe, thought Mr. Reeder, and it is difficult to breathe noiselessly in a silent corridor in the dead of night. Conscious that he himself was a little too silent for a supposedly sleeping man, he emitted a lifelike snore and gurgle which might be expected from a middle-aged man in the first stages of slumber.

Something touched the end of the rod, pushing it aside. Mr. Reeder turned the switch and a blinding ray of light leapt from the lamp and focused in a circle on the opposite wall of the corridor.

===Chinese===
两点整，分秒不差，他醒了，坐到床沿上，对着灯光眨眼。他打开一只旅行箱，取出一只小木盒，从里面拿出一具酒精炉和一套沏茶的行头。他点着小灯，趁那把小洋铁壶烧水的当口，去浴室脱了衣服，把打颤的身子沉进一盆冷水里。再回来时已穿戴整齐，壶里的水正滚着。

里德先生是个做事极有章法的人；而且，是个谨慎的人。他一辈子对牛奶怀着戒心。他惯于清早遛达在郊外的街上，看那些挂在门环上的奶罐、搁在门槛角落里的奶瓶，琢磨这桩轻飘飘的送奶风俗，给存心犯罪的人提供了何等浩大的批量谋杀的可能。他估算过，一个手脚麻利的凶手，若按部就班地干，一个月就能把伦敦杀掉十分之一。

他的茶不加牛奶，嚼了块饼干，然后有条不紊地收拾掉酒精炉和壶，从手提包里取出一双厚毡底拖鞋穿上。他在旅行箱里找到一截硬橡胶短棍——到了行家手里，那是跟刀子一样致命的武器。他把它放进外套内袋。他的手又伸进箱里，摸出一件东西，看着像一只薄橡胶海绵袋，只是上面嵌着两方云母片和一个小金属喷嘴。他拿着它翻来覆去掂量，犹豫不决，最后还是放回了箱里。接着摸出来的是那把短粗的勃朗宁手枪，里德先生对它颇不待见，因为在他看来，除非到了最绝望的境地，火器的价值从来是可疑的。

最后取出来的是一根中空竹竿，里面还套着一根，这正是他曾经表示想要的那根钓竿。细的那根末端有一个弹簧环；他把两截拧在一起，往弹簧环上装了一盏小小的电筒灯，再把细电线仔细穿过竿上的导眼，接到手柄上一个小开关——就在寻常渔夫握竿的地方附近。他试了试开关，很满意；一切就绪后，他环视房间最后一圈，熄掉了桌上的灯。

若在大天白日里，他这副模样多少有些滑稽：盘腿坐在床上，长长的钓竿伸到房间中央，架在床尾板上；可此刻，J. G. 里德先生全无滑稽之感，何况，也没有旁观者。

他不时把钓竿左右摇摆，像垂钓者重新抛竿。他毫无睡意，两耳调得极灵，分辨得出夜间的正常声响——树叶的沙沙、微风的轻哼——和那些只能出自人类活动的动静。

他坐了半个多小时，钓竿来回游移；突然，他察觉一股冷风从门那边吹来。他什么声音也没听见——连锁舌的一声轻响都没有；可他知道，那扇门大开着。

他悄没声地把竿收回来，直到离开床柱，再转向门口一寸寸放出去，直到竿头离他坐处不过两码——这时他一只脚已踏在地上，随时准备跃起或卧倒，全看事态怎么吩咐。

竿头没有碰到任何阻碍。里德屏住呼吸——听着。外面的走廊铺着厚地毯。他不指望听见脚步声。可是人总要喘气，里德先生想，而在深夜死寂的走廊里，要喘气喘得不出声，谈何容易。他意识到，自己作为一个据称睡着的人，未免太安静了些，便发出一声逼真的鼾声和喉音——正像一个中年人初入睡时该有的动静。

什么东西碰了竿头一下，把它拨向一边。里德先生一拧开关，一道刺目的光从灯里蹿出，在走廊对面的墙上聚成一个光圈。

===Original===
The door was open, but there was nothing human in sight.

And then, despite his wonderful nerve, his flesh began to go goosey, and a cold sensation tingled up his spine. Somebody was there﻿—hiding﻿—waiting for the man who carried the lamp, as they thought, to emerge.

Reaching out at a full arm’s length, he thrust the end of the rod through the doorway into the corridor.

*Swish!*

Something struck the rod and snapped it. The lamp fell on the floor, lens uppermost, and flooded the ceiling of the corridor. In an instant Reeder was off the bed, moving swiftly, till he came to the cover afforded by the wide-open door. Through the crack he had a limited view of what might happen outside.

There was a deadly silence. In the hall downstairs a clock ticked solemnly, whirred, and struck the quarter to three. But there was no movement; nothing came within the range of the upturned lamp, until﻿—

He had just a momentary flash of vision. The thin white face; the hairy lips parted in a grin; wild dirty white hair, and a bald crown; a short bristle of white beard; a claw-like hand reaching for the lamp﻿—

Pistol or rubber? Mr. Reeder elected the rubber. As the hand closed over the lamp, he left the cover of the room and struck. He heard a snarl like that of a wild beast; then the lamp was extinguished as the apparition staggered back, snapping the thin wire.

The corridor was in darkness. He struck again and missed; the violence of the stroke was such that he overbalanced and fell on one knee, and the truncheon flew from his grasp. He threw out his hand, gripped an arm, and with a quick jerk brought his capture into the room and switched on the light.

A round, soft hand, covered with a silken sleeve﻿—

As the lights leapt to life, he found himself looking into the pale face of Olga Crewe!

===Chinese===
门开着，却看不见一个人影。

接着，尽管他胆力过人，他的皮肉还是起了鸡皮疙瘩，一股寒意顺着脊梁往上钻。有人在那儿——藏着——等着那个提灯的人，照他们的想法，露面出来。

他伸直手臂，把竿头从门口捅进走廊。

*嗖！*

什么东西击中钓竿，把它击断了。灯摔在地上，镜片朝上，把走廊的天花板照得一片通明。转瞬间里德已下了床，疾步移动，直到隐蔽到那扇大开房门提供的掩蔽之后。透过门缝，他对外面可能发生的事只能窥见一隅。

一片死寂。楼下门厅里，一座钟庄严地滴答着，嗡的一声，敲了三点差一刻。但没有动静；没有什么进入那盏仰面朝天的灯的照野，直到——

他只来得及瞥见一瞬。一张瘦削的白脸；多毛的嘴唇咧开一个狞笑；狂乱肮脏的白发，秃的头顶；一小茬白胡茬；一只爪子般的手伸向那盏灯——

手枪还是橡胶棍？里德先生选了橡胶棍。就在那只手罩住灯的当口，他离开房间的掩蔽，一棍击出。他听见一声野兽似的咆哮；随即那幻影踉跄后退，扯断了细线，灯灭了。

走廊陷入黑暗。他又击一棍，落了空；那一击势道太猛，他失了平衡，单膝摔倒，警棍脱手飞了出去。他猛地伸手，攥住一条胳膊，顺势一带，把猎物拽进房间，拧亮了灯。

一只圆润绵软的手，覆着一只绸袖——

灯光乍亮，他发现自己正对着奥尔加·克鲁那张苍白的脸！

## X / 十

===Original===
For a moment they stared at one another, she fearfully, he amazed. Olga Crewe!
Then he became conscious that he was still gripping her arm, and let it drop. The arm fascinated Mr. Reeder﻿—he scarcely looked at anything else.
“I am very sorry,” said Mr. Reeder. “Where did you come from?”
Her lips were quivering; she tried to speak, but no words came. Then she mastered her momentary paralysis and began to speak, slowly, laboriously:
“I﻿—heard﻿—a noise﻿—in﻿—the﻿—corridor﻿—and﻿—came﻿—out. A noise﻿—I﻿—was﻿—frightened.”

===Chinese===
有那么一瞬间，两人直瞪瞪地望着彼此——她满怀惊惶，他满心讶异。竟是奥尔加·克鲁（Olga Crewe）！
随即他发觉自己仍攥着她的胳膊，便松手放开了。这条胳膊令里德先生着了迷——旁的他几乎一概没瞧。
“非常抱歉，”里德先生说，“您是从哪儿来的？”
她的嘴唇哆嗦着；她想说话，却吐不出一个字。随后她克制住那一阵短暂的僵麻，开口说起话来，缓慢而吃力：
“我——听见——走廊里——有——响动——就——出来了。一个响动——我——吓坏了。”

===Original===
She was rubbing her arm mechanically; he saw a red welt where his hand had gripped. The wonder was that he had not broken her arm.
“Is﻿—anything﻿—wrong?”
Every word was created and articulated painfully. She seemed to be considering its formation before her tongue gave it sound.
“Where is the light switch in the hall?” asked Mr. Reeder. This was a more practical matter﻿—he lost interest in her arm.
“Opposite my room.”

===Chinese===
她机械地揉着胳膊；他看见自己手攥过的地方起了一道红痕。没把她的胳膊攥断，已是奇事一桩。
“出——出事——了吗？”
每个字都孕育得艰难，吐得吃力。仿佛舌头给它发音之前，她都要先把这字的形状掂量一番。
“门厅里的电灯开关在哪儿？”里德先生问。这才是更要紧的实际问题——他对那条胳膊失去了兴趣。
“在我房间对面。”

===Original===
“Turn it on,” he said, and she obeyed meekly.
Only when the corridor was illuminated did he step out of his room, and even then in some doubt, if the Browning in his hand meant anything.
“Is anything wrong?” she asked again. By now she had taken command of herself. A little colour had come to her white face, but the live eyes were still beholding terrible visions.
“Did you see anything in the passage?” he answered.

===Chinese===
“把它打开。”他说，她乖乖照办了。
直到走廊被照亮，他才跨出房门，而且即便这时也仍有几分迟疑——倘使他手里那把勃朗宁手枪能说明什么的话。
“出什么事了吗？”她又问。此刻她已恢复自持。苍白的脸上透出些许血色，可那双鲜活的眼睛仍在凝望可怕的幻象。
“您在过道里看见什么了吗？”他答道。

===Original===
She shook her head slowly.
“No, I saw nothing﻿—nothing. I heard a noise and I came out.”
She was lying﻿—he did not trouble to doubt this. She had had time to pull on her slippers and find the flimsy wrap she wore, and the fight had not lasted more than two seconds. Moreover, he had not heard her door open; therefore it had been open all the time, and she had been spectator or audience of all that had happened.
He went down the corridor, retrieved his rubber truncheon, and came back to her. She was half standing, half leaning against the door post, rubbing her arm. She was staring past him so intently that he looked round, though there was nothing to be seen.
“You hurt me,” she said simply.

===Chinese===
她缓缓摇头。
“没有，我什么也没看见——什么也没有。我听见响动，就出来了。”
她在撒谎——他连怀疑的工夫都懒得费。她来得及趿上拖鞋、来得及寻出身上那件薄薄的晨衣，而那场搏斗前后不过两秒钟。何况，他没有听见她的房门开动；可见那扇门一直开着，方才发生的一切，她从头到尾都是旁观者、旁听者。
他沿走廊走过去，捡回他的橡皮警棍，再回到她跟前。她半站半倚在门柱上，揉着胳膊。她直勾勾地盯着他身后的某处，盯得那样专注，害得他也回头看了看——尽管那儿什么都没有。
“您弄疼我了。”她简单地说。

===Original===
“Did I? I’m sorry.”
The mark on the white flesh had gone blue, and Mr. Reeder was naturally a sympathetic man. Yet, if the truth be told, there was nothing of sorrow in his mind at that moment. Regret, yes. But the regret had nothing to do with her hurt.
“I think you’d better go to your bed, young lady. My nightmare is ended. I hope yours will end as quickly, though I shall be surprised if it does. Mine is for the moment; yours, unless I am greatly mistaken, is for life!”
Her dark, inscrutable eyes did not leave his face as she spoke.
“I think it must have been a nightmare,” she said. “It will last all my life? I think it will!”

===Chinese===
“是吗？对不起。”
白皙肌肤上的那道痕已经泛青，而里德先生天生是个心软的人。然而，说句实话，那一刻他心里没有半分难过。遗憾嘛，有的。可那遗憾，与她的疼痛毫无干系。
“我看您最好回床上去，小姐。我的噩梦已经醒了。但愿您的那场也能醒得一样快——果真醒得了的话，我倒要吃惊了。我的一场只在一时；您的那场，除非我大错特错，是要缠您一辈子的！”
她说话时，那双黑沉沉、深不可测的眼睛始终没有离开他的脸。
“我想那准是场噩梦，”她说，“要缠我一辈子？我想会的！”

===Original===
With a nod she turned away, and presently he heard her door close and the lock fasten.
Mr. Reeder went back to the far side of his bed, pulled up a chair and sat down. He did not attempt to close the door. Whilst his room was in darkness, and the corridor lighted, he did not expect a repetition of his bad and substantial dream.
The rubber truncheon was a mistake, he admitted regretfully. He wished he had not such a repugnance to a noisier weapon. He laid the pistol on the cover of the bed within reach of his hand. If the bad dream came again﻿—
Voices!

===Chinese===
她点点头，转身走了，不多时他听见她的房门关上，锁舌咬合。
里德先生踱回床的另一头，拖过一把椅子坐下。他没有去关房门。只要他的房间黑着、走廊亮着，他料想那场糟糕而结实的梦不会再重演。
橡皮警棍是个失着，他懊悔地承认。他但愿自己对吵闹些的武器不至于这般反感。他把手枪放到床罩上，搁在自己伸手可及的地方。倘若那场噩梦再来——
人声！

===Original===
The murmur of a whispered colloquy and a fierce, hissing whisper that dominated the others. Not in the corridor, but in the hall below. He tiptoed to the door and listened.
Somebody laughed under his breath, a strange, bloodcurdling little laugh; then he heard a key turn and a door open, and a voice demand:
“Who is there?”
It was Margaret. Her room faced the head of the stairs, he remembered. Slipping the pistol into his pocket, he ran round the end of the bed and into the corridor. She was standing by the banisters, looking down into the dark. The whispered voices had ceased. She saw him out of the corner of her eye and turned with a start.
“What is wrong, Mr. Reeder? Who put the corridor light on? I heard somebody speaking in the vestibule.”

===Chinese===
一阵交头接耳的絮语，其中一个凶狠的、嘶嘶作响的耳语压过了其余所有声音。不在走廊里，而在楼下的门厅里。他踮着脚走到门边，侧耳倾听。
有人低声笑了，笑声怪异，令人毛骨悚然；接着他听见钥匙转动、一扇门打开，一个声音喝问：
“谁在那儿？”
是玛格丽特。他记得，她的房间正对着楼梯口。他把手枪塞进口袋，绕过床尾冲进走廊。她正站在楼梯扶手旁，朝下面的黑暗里张望。窃窃私语声已经止息。她从眼角里瞥见他，吓了一跳似的转过身来。
“出什么事了，里德先生？走廊的灯是谁开的？我听见有人在前厅里说话。”

===Original===
“It was only I.”
His smile would in ordinary circumstances have been very reassuring, but now she was frightened, childishly frightened. She had an insane desire to cling to him and weep.
“Something has been happening here,” she said. “I’ve been lying in bed listening and haven’t had the courage to get up. I’m horribly scared, Mr. Reeder.”
He beckoned her to him, and as she came, wondering, he slipped past her and took her place at the banisters. She saw him lean over and the light from a hand lamp sweep the space below.
“There’s nobody there,” he said airily.

===Chinese===
“只有我。”
换作平常，他这样的微笑本会让人心里踏实得很，可此刻她吓坏了，孩子气地吓坏了。她有一股疯狂的冲动，想紧紧抓住他，放声大哭。
“这儿一直在出事，”她说，“我躺在床上一直听着，却没有勇气起来。我害怕极了，里德先生。”
他招手让她过来，待她疑惑地走近时，他从她身边一闪而过，占据了她在扶手旁的位置。她看见他俯下身去，一盏手提灯的光扫过楼下的空间。
“下面没有人。”他轻描淡写地说。

===Original===
She was whiter than he had ever seen her.
“There *was* somebody there,” she insisted. “I heard footsteps on the tiled paving after you put on your flashlamp.”
“Probably Mrs. Burton,” he suggested. “I thought I heard her voice.”
And now arrived a newcomer on the scene. Mr. Daver had appeared at the end of the corridor. He wore a flowered silk dressing gown buttoned up to his chin.
“Whatever is the matter, Miss Belman?” he asked. “Don’t tell me that he tried to get into *your* window! I’m afraid you’re going to tell me that! I hope you’re not, but I’m afraid you will! Dear me, what an unpleasant thing to happen!”

===Chinese===
她的脸色比他以往见过的任何一次都更白。
“下面*确实*有人，”她坚持道，“您打开手电之后，我听见瓷砖地面上有脚步声。”
“多半是伯顿太太（Mrs. Burton），”他猜道，“我好像听见了她的声音。”
这时，场上又来了一位新角色。达弗先生（Mr. Daver）出现在走廊尽头。他穿一件花绸睡袍，一直扣到下巴。
“究竟出了什么事，贝尔曼小姐？”他问，“可别告诉我说，他想钻进*您的*窗户！我怕您就要这么说了！我希望不是，可我怕就是！天哪，这是多么叫人不快的事！”

===Original===
“What has happened?” asked Mr. Reeder.
“I don’t know, but I have an uncomfortable feeling that somebody has been trying to break into this house,” said Mr. Daver.
He was genuinely agitated; the girl could almost hear his teeth chatter.
“I heard somebody trying the catch of my window and looked out, and I’ll swear I saw﻿—something! What a dreadful thing to happen! I have half a mind to telephone for the police.”
“An excellent idea,” murmured Mr. Reeder, suddenly his old deferential and agreeable self. “You were asleep, I suppose, when you heard the noise?”

===Chinese===
“出什么事了？”里德先生问。
“我不知道，但我隐隐不安，觉得一直有人在试图闯进这所宅子。”达弗先生说。
他是真的惶惶不安；姑娘几乎听得见他牙齿打战。
“我听见有人拨弄我窗户的搭扣，往外看了看，我敢起誓，我看见了——个什么东西！这是多么可怕的事！我简直想去打电话叫警察了。”
“好主意。”里德先生喃喃道，倏然又摆出他平日那副恭谨和气的模样，“您听见响动的时候，想必正在睡觉吧？”

===Original===
Mr. Daver hesitated.
“Not exactly asleep,” he said. “Between sleeping and waking. I was very restless tonight for some reason.”
He put up his hand to his throat, his dressing gown had gaped for a second. He was not quite quick enough.
“You were probably restless,” said Mr. Reeder softly, “because you omitted to take off your collar and tie. I know of nothing more disturbing.”
Mr. Daver made a characteristic grimace.

===Chinese===
达弗先生迟疑了一下。
“也不算睡着了，”他说，“半睡半醒吧。今晚不知怎么，我翻来覆去睡不安稳。”
他抬手掩向喉咙——他的睡袍敞开过一秒钟。他的动作还是不够快。
“您睡不安稳，”里德先生轻声说，“多半是因为您忘了摘下硬领和领带。我想不出还有比这更叫人心绪不宁的事了。”
达弗先生做了个他特有的怪相。

===Original===
“I dressed myself rather hurriedly﻿—” he began.
“Better to undress yourself hurriedly,” chided Mr. Reeder, almost playfully. “People who go to bed in stiff white collars occasionally choke themselves to death. And there is sorrow in the home of the cheated hangman. Your burglar probably saved your life.”
Daver made as though to speak, suddenly retreated, and slammed the door.
Margaret was looking at Mr. Reeder apprehensively.
“What is the mystery﻿—was there a burglar? Oh, please tell me the truth! I shall get hysterical if you don’t!”

===Chinese===
“我穿衣服穿得太匆忙了——”他开口道。
“匆忙脱掉更好。”里德先生责备道，语气近乎俏皮，“戴着硬挺的白领子上床的人，偶尔会把自己活活憋死。而被抢了生意的绞刑吏家里，也要愁云惨雾。您那位窃贼多半救了您一命。”
达弗张口似要说话，忽然缩了回去，砰的一声摔上了门。
玛格丽特忧心忡忡地望着里德先生。
“到底有什么蹊跷——刚才是有贼吗？哦，请告诉我实话！不然我要歇斯底里了！”

===Original===
“The truth,” said Mr. Reeder, his eyes twinkling, “is very nearly what that curious man told you﻿—there was somebody in the house, somebody who had no right to be here, but I think he has gone, and you can go to bed without the slightest anxiety.”
She looked at him oddly.
“Are you going to bed, too?”
“In a very few moments,” said Mr. Reeder cheerfully.

===Chinese===
“实话，”里德先生说，眼里闪着光，“跟那位古怪先生告诉您的相差无几——宅子里是有过一个人，一个无权待在这儿的人，不过我想他已经走了，您可以回床上去了，丝毫不必担心。”
她古怪地看着他。
“您也去睡吗？”
“过一会儿就去。”里德先生欣然说。

===Original===
She held out her hand with an impulsive gesture. He took it in both of his.
“You are my idea of a guardian angel,” she smiled, though she was near to tears.
“I’ve never heard,” said Mr. Reeder, “of guardian angels with side whiskers.”
It was a mean advantage to take of her, yet he was ridiculously pleased as he repeated his little jeu d’esprit to himself in the seclusion of his room.

===Chinese===
她冲动地伸出手来。他用双手握住了它。
“您就是我心中的守护天使。”她微笑道，虽然她几乎要落泪了。
“我还从没听说过，”里德先生说，“长着连鬓胡的守护天使。”
这样占一个姑娘的便宜，实在有失厚道；可当他回到自己房中的清静里，独自反复回味那句小小的妙语时，竟得意得有些可笑。

## XI / 十一

===Original===
Mr. Reeder closed the door, put on the lights, and set himself to unravel the inexplicable mystery of its opening. Before he went to bed he had shot home the bolt, had turned the key in the lock, and the key was still on the inside. It struck him, as he turned it, that he had never heard a lock that moved so silently or a bolt that slipped so easily into its groove. Both lock and bolt had been recently oiled. He began a scrutiny of the inside face of the door, and provided a simple solution to the somewhat baffling incident of its opening.
The door consisted of eight panels, carved in small lozenge-shaped ornaments. The panel immediately above the lock moved slightly when he pressed it, but it was a long time before he found the tiny spring which held it in place. When that was found, the panel opened like a miniature door. He could thrust his hand through the aperture and slide back the bolt with the greatest ease.
There was nothing very unusual or sinister about this. He knew that many hotels and boarding houses had methods by which a door could be unlocked from the outside﻿—a very necessary precaution in certain eventualities. Mr. Reeder wondered whether he would find a similar safety panel on the door of Margaret Belman’s room.

===Chinese===
里德先生关上房门，点亮灯，着手解开这扇门何以被打开的难解之谜。上床之前，他早已把门闩推到头，在锁孔里拧转了钥匙，而钥匙此刻仍插在门内侧。转动钥匙时他忽然想到，他从未听见过哪把锁开合得如此无声无息，也没见过哪根门闩滑进闩槽这般顺畅。锁和闩都是新近上过油的。他细察起门板的内面，为这桩颇为费解的门户洞开事件找到了一个简单的解答。
这扇门由八块格板拼成，板上雕着小小的菱形花纹。紧挨着锁上方的那块格板，一按便微微活动，可他找了许久，才发现固定它的小小弹簧。找到之后，那块格板便像一扇微型小门似的开启了。他可以将手从开口里探进去，轻而易举地把门闩拉回。
这里面并没有什么特别出奇或者凶险之处。他知道，许多旅馆和供膳公寓都有从外头把房门打开的机关——在某些意外情势下，这是极必要的预防之策。里德先生暗自琢磨，玛格丽特·贝尔曼房间的门上，是否也装着一块类似的安全格板。

===Original===
By the time he had completed his inspection, it was daylight, and, pulling back the curtains, he drew a chair to the window and made a survey of as much of the grounds as lay within his line of vision.
There were two or three matters which were puzzling him. If Larmes Keep was the headquarters of the Flack gang, in what manner and for what reason had Olga Crewe been brought into the confederation? He judged her age at twenty-four; she had been a constant visitor, if not a resident, at Larmes Keep for at least ten years, and he knew enough of the ways of the underworld to realize that they did not employ children. Also she had been to a public school of some kind, and that would have absorbed at least four of the ten years. Mr. Reeder shook his head in doubt.
Nothing would happen now until dark, he decided, and, stretching himself upon the bed, he pulled the coverlet over him and slept till a tapping at the door announced the coming of the housemaid with his morning tea. She was a round-faced woman, just past her first youth, with a disagreeable cockney accent and the brusque and familiar manner of one who was an indispensable part of the establishment. Mr. Reeder remembered that the girl had waited on him at dinner.
“Why, sir, you haven’t undressed!” she said.
“I seldom undress,” said Mr. Reeder, sitting up and taking the tea from her. “It is such a waste of time. For no sooner are your clothes off than it is necessary to put them on again.”

===Chinese===
待他查勘完毕，天已大亮。他拉开窗帘，把一把椅子拖到窗前，将视野之内的园中景致检阅了一番。
有两三桩事正叫他纳闷。倘若拉尔姆宅（Larmes Keep）是弗拉克团伙的总部，奥尔加·克鲁（Olga Crewe）又是以什么方式、为何缘故被拉进这个同盟里来的？他估量她的年纪在二十四岁上下；她若非常住，至少也是拉尔姆宅十年来的常客，而他对黑道的行情知道得够多，明白他们是不雇孩子的。何况她还进过某种公学，这又要占去那十年里的至少四年。里德先生满腹狐疑地摇了摇头。
他断定，天黑之前不会再有什么动静，于是往床上一躺，拉过床罩盖在身上，一觉睡到叩门声起——女仆送早茶来了。她是个圆脸盘的女人，青春年华方过，一口伦敦土腔听着难受，举止粗率而自来熟，一副这宅子里少不了她的派头。里德先生记起，昨晚晚餐伺候他的正是这姑娘。
“哎呀，先生，您衣服都没脱呀！”她说。
“我很少脱衣服，”里德先生说着坐起身来，从她手里接过茶，“那太浪费时间啦。衣服才刚脱下，转眼不又得穿上。”

===Original===
She looked at him hard, but he did not smile.
“You’re a detective, ain’t you? Everybody at the cottage knows that you are. What have you come down about?”
Now Mr. Reeder could afford to smile cryptically. There was a suppressed anxiety in the girl’s voice.
“It is not for me, my dear young lady, to disclose your employer’s business.”
“He brought you down? Well, he’s got a nerve!”
Mr. Reeder put his finger to his lips.
“About the candlesticks?”

===Chinese===
她直勾勾地打量他，可他没有笑。
“您是个侦探，不是吗？下房里人人知道。您下来是查什么案的？”
这下里德先生尽可以讳莫如深地一笑。姑娘的嗓音里，藏着一股按捺不住的焦灼。
“您东家的公事，可轮不到我这张嘴来泄露，亲爱的小姐。”
“是他把您请下来的？嗬，他脸皮可真厚！”
里德先生把手指抵在唇上。
“是为了烛台的事？”

===Original===
He nodded.
“He still thinks somebody in the house took them?”
Her face was very red, her eyes snapped angrily. Here was exposed one of the minor scandals of the hotel.
It was not an uninteresting sidelight. For if ever guilt was written on a woman’s face it was on hers. What these candlesticks were and how they disappeared, Mr. Reeder could guess. Petty larceny runs in well-defined channels.
“Well, you can tell him from me﻿—” she began shrilly, and he raised a solemn hand.
“Keep the matter to yourself﻿—regard me as your friend,” he begged.
He was in his lighter moments a most mischievous man, a weakness that few suspected in Mr. J. G. Reeder. Moreover, he wanted badly some inside information about the household, and he had an idea that this infuriated girl who flounced out and slammed the door behind her would supply him with that information. In his most optimistic moments he could not dream that in her raw hands she held the secret of Larmes Keep.

===Chinese===
他点了点头。
“他还当是宅子里的什么人拿的？”
她的脸涨得通红，眼睛里冒着火。旅馆里一桩小小的丑闻，就此抖落了出来。
这倒不是个无趣的侧面材料。因为倘说罪状会写在女人脸上，那正是写在她的脸上。那几只烛台是什么东西、又是怎么消失的，里德先生猜得出个大概。小偷小摸，向来有它固定的路数。
“好哇，您替我捎句话给他——”她尖声开了腔，他郑重地举起一只手。
“这事您只搁在自己心里——把我当作您的朋友。”他恳求道。
他心境轻松的时候，本是个最爱恶作剧的人——J. G. 里德的这一弱点，鲜有人疑心到。再说，他也极想要一些这户人家的内情，而且他有种预感：这个气冲冲夺门而出、把门砰地摔在身后的姑娘，自会把这些内情送到他嘴里。哪怕在他最乐观的时刻，他也梦想不到，拉尔姆宅的秘密竟攥在她那双粗手手里。

===Original===
As soon as he came down, Mr. Reeder decided to go to Daver’s office; he was curious to learn the true story of the missing candlesticks. The sound of an angry voice reached him, and as his hand was raised to knock at the door, it was opened by somebody who was holding the handle on the inside, and he heard a woman’s angry voice.
“You’ve treated me shabbily: that’s all I can say to you, Mr. Daver! I’ve been working for you five years and I’ve never said a word about your business to anybody! And now you bring a detective down to spy on me! I won’t be treated as if I was a thief or something! If you think that’s behaving fair and square, after all I’ve done for you, and minding my own business.﻿ ﻿… Yes, I know I’ve been well paid, but I could get just as much money somewhere else. I’ve got my pride, Mr. Daver, the same as you have, and I think you’ve been very underhand, the way you’ve treated me. I’ll go tonight, don’t you worry!”
The door was flung open and a red-faced girl of twenty-five flounced out and dashed past the eavesdropper, scarcely noticing him in her fury. The door shut behind her; evidently Mr. Daver was in as bad a temper as the girl﻿—a fortunate circumstance, as it proved, and Mr. Reeder decided it might be inadvisable to advertise that he had overheard the whole or part of the conversation.

===Chinese===
一下楼，里德先生便决定到达弗的办公室去；那几只失踪的烛台究竟有怎样的内情，他好奇心切。一阵怒气冲冲的人声传进他耳朵，他抬手正要敲门，门却由里面握着把手的人拉开了，他听见一个女人含怒的嗓音。
“您待我太刻薄了：达弗先生，我只有这一句话跟您说！我给您做了五年工，您的事我对谁都没吐过一个字！如今您倒弄了个侦探下来盯我！我才不要被人当成贼还是什么东西似的！我替您做了这么多，又一向安分守己，您要是觉得这样还算光明正大，那您就想吧。……是的，我知道工钱不薄，可我到别处一样挣得着这么多钱。我也有我的自尊，达弗先生，跟您一样，而且我觉得您这一手太阴了。我今夜就走，您甭操心！”
门被猛地甩开，一个红脸盘的二十五岁姑娘冲了出来，从这位偷听者身边疾掠而过，盛怒之下几乎没有瞧见他。门在她身后关上；显而易见，达弗先生同这姑娘一样火气不小——事后证明，这倒是桩走运的事，于是里德先生拿定主意，自己偷听到了这场谈话的全部也好、一部分也罢，还是不宜声张。

===Original===
When he strolled out into the sunlit grounds, of all the people who had been disturbed during the night he was the brightest and showed the least sign of fatigue. He met the Rev. Mr. Dean and the Colonel, who was carrying a golf bag, and they bade him a gruff good morning. The Colonel, he thought, was a little haggard; Mr. Dean gave him a scowl as he passed.
Walking up and down the lawn, he examined the front of the house with a critical eye. The lines of the Keep were very definite: harsh and angular; not even the Tudor windows, which at some remote period had been introduced to its stony face, could disguise its ancient grimness.
Turning an angle of the house, he reached the strip of lawn that faced his own window. Behind the lawn was a mass of rhododendron bushes, which might serve a useful purpose, but which in certain circumstances might also be a danger point.
Immediately beneath his window was an angle of the drawing room, a circumstance that gave him cause for satisfaction. Mr. Reeder’s experience favoured a bedroom that was above a public apartment.
He went back on his tracks and came to the other end of the lawn. Those three windows, brightly curtained, were evidently Mr. Daver’s private suite. Beneath them, the wall was black, the actual stone being obscured by a thick growth of ivy. He wondered what this lightless and doorless space contained.

===Chinese===
待他踱步到阳光朗照的园子里，昨夜被惊动过的众人当中，数他最精神，最看不出倦色。他遇见了迪恩牧师（the Rev. Mr. Dean）和提着高尔夫球袋的上校，两人没好气地同他道了早安。上校的模样，他觉得，有几分憔悴；迪恩先生走过时，则白了他一眼。
他在草坪上踱来踱去，用一副挑剔的眼光打量宅子的正面。主堡的线条斩截分明：冷硬而多棱角；纵然那几扇都铎式的窗子曾在某个遥远年代嵌进它石头的脸面，也遮掩不住它自古而来的狰狞。
绕过宅子的一角，他走到正对自己窗前的那条草坪上。草坪后面是一大片杜鹃花丛，这或许可以派上大用场，可在某些情势下，也可能成为一处险地。
他窗子底下正是一处客厅的拐角，这一情形令他颇感满意。照里德先生的经验，楼下是公共厅堂的卧房，最合他的心意。
他循原路折返，走到草坪的另一头。那三扇窗子挂着鲜亮的窗帘，显然是达弗先生的私人套间。窗子底下的墙面黑沉沉的，真正的石体被一层茂密的常春藤遮没了。他不免纳闷，那片不见天光、没有门洞的地方究竟装着些什么。

===Original===
As he returned to the front of the house he saw Margaret Belman. She was standing in front of the doorway, shading her eyes from the sun, evidently searching her limited landscape for somebody. Seeing him, she came quickly to meet him.
“Oh, there you are!” she said, with a sigh of relief. “I wondered what had happened to you﻿—you didn’t come down to breakfast.”
She looked a little peaked, he thought. Evidently she had not rounded off the night as agreeably as he.
“I haven’t slept since I saw you,” she said, answering his unspoken question. “What happened, Mr. Reeder? Did somebody really try to get into the house﻿—a burglar?”
“I think somebody tried, and I think succeeded,” said Mr. Reeder carefully. “Burglaries happen even in﻿—um﻿—hotels, Miss﻿—um﻿—Margaret. Has Mr. Daver notified the police?”
She shook her head.
“I don’t know. He has been telephoning all the morning﻿—I went to his room just now and it was locked, but I heard his voice. And, Mr. Reeder, you didn’t tell me the terrible thing that happened the night I left London. I saw it in the newspaper this morning.”

===Chinese===
他绕回宅子正面时，望见了玛格丽特·贝尔曼。她正站在门前，抬手遮着阳光，显然在她那视野有限的天地里搜寻着什么人。一见他，她便快步迎了上来。
“哦，您在这儿哪！”她舒了口气说，“我还担心您出了什么事——您没下来吃早饭。”
她瞧着有点形容憔悴，他想。显然，她这一夜收的梢，不如他惬意。
“自打见过您，我就没合过眼，”她答着他没问出口的问题，“到底出什么事了，里德先生？当真有人想闯进宅子——是个贼吗？”
“我看是有人试了，而且我看是成了，”里德先生斟酌着说，“盗窃案嘛，就连——嗯——旅馆里也会有的，小姐——嗯——玛格丽特。达弗先生报警察了吗？”
她摇了摇头。
“我不知道。他一早上都在打电话——我刚才去他房间，房门锁着，可我听得见他的声音。还有，里德先生，我离开伦敦那晚出的那桩可怕的事，您没告诉我。今天早上我在报上看到了。”

===Original===
“Terrible thing?”
J. G. Reeder was puzzled. Almost he had forgotten the adventure of the spring gun.
“Oh, you mean the little joke?”
“Joke!” she said, shocked.
“Criminals have a perverted sense of humour,” said Mr. Reeder airily. “The whole thing was﻿—um﻿—an elaborate jest designed to frighten me. One expects such things. They are the examination papers which are set to test one’s intelligence from time to time.”
“But who did it?” she asked.
Mr. Reeder’s gaze wandered absently over the placid countryside. She had a feeling that it bored him even to recall so trivial an incident in a busy life.

===Chinese===
“可怕的事？”
J. G. 里德纳闷了。那桩弹簧枪的奇遇，他差不多已经忘了。
“哦，您是说那个小小的玩笑？”
“玩笑！”她说，骇然了。
“罪犯的幽默感是走了岔道的，”里德先生轻描淡写地说，“整件事是——嗯——一个煞费苦心的玩笑，存心吓唬我。这种事嘛，尽在料中。它们就是不时拿来考一考一个人智力的考卷。”
“可那是谁干的呢？”她问。
里德先生的目光心不在焉地掠过平静的乡野。她有种感觉：在忙碌的一生里，连这样一桩小事都要回想，简直叫他腻烦。

===Original===
“Our young friend,” he said suddenly, and, following the direction of his eyes, she saw Olga Crewe.
She was wearing a dark gray knitted suit and a big black hat that shaded her face, and there was nothing of embarrassment in the half smile with which she greeted her fellow guest.
“Good morning, Mr. Reeder. I think we have met before this morning.” She rubbed her arm good-humouredly.
Mr. Reeder was all apologies.
“I don’t even know now what happened,” she said, and Margaret Belman learned for the first time what had happened before she had made her appearance.
“I never thought you were so strong﻿—look!” Olga Crewe pulled back her sleeve and showed a big blue-black patch on her forearm, cutting short his expression of remorse with a little laugh.
“Have you shown Mr. Reeder all the attractions of the estate?” she asked, a hint of sarcasm in her tone. “I almost expected to find you at the bathing pool this morning.”

===Chinese===
“咱们那位年轻的朋友，”他忽然说。她顺着他的目光望去，看见了奥尔加·克鲁。
她穿一身深灰色的针织套装，戴一顶遮着脸的大黑帽，迎向这位同宅的客人半含笑意，没有半分局促。
“早安，里德先生。我想今天早上，咱们已经打过照面了。”她心情颇好地揉着胳膊。
里德先生连声道着歉。
“我到现在还不知道出了什么事呢，”她说。于是，她露面之前发生的那一切，玛格丽特·贝尔曼头一回听说了。
“我从没想到您力气这么大——瞧！”奥尔加·克鲁捋起袖子，给他看小臂上一大块青紫的淤痕，一声轻笑截住了他满脸的歉疚。
“您领里德先生看遍这产业的名胜了吗？”她问，话音里带一丝讥诮，“今天早上，我几乎料定会在游泳池边找到您。”

===Original===
“I didn’t even know there was a bathing pool,” said Mr. Reeder. “In fact, after my terrible scare last night, this﻿—um﻿—beautiful house has assumed so sinister an aspect that I expect to bathe in nothing less dramatic than blood!”
She was not amused. He saw her eyes close quickly and she shivered a little.
“How gruesome you are! Come along, Miss Belman.”
Inwardly Margaret resented the tone, which was almost a command, but she walked by their side. Clear of the house, Olga stopped and pointed.
“You must see the well. Are you interested in old things?” asked Olga, as she led the way to the shrubbery.
“I am more interested in new things, especially new experiences,” said Mr. Reeder, quite gaily. “And new people fascinate me!”
Again that quick, frightened smile of hers.
“Then you should be having the time of your life, Mr. Reeder,” she said, “for you’re meeting people here whom you’ve never met before.”

===Chinese===
“我压根儿不知道还有个游泳池，”里德先生说，“说实话，经过昨夜那场可怕的惊吓，这——嗯——美丽的宅子在我眼里已经换了一副阴森面目，我甚至预料，往后自己沐浴所用之物，戏剧性不会低于鲜血！”
她并不觉得有趣。他看见她的眼睛飞快地闭了一下，身子微微一颤。
“您这个人真叫人毛骨悚然！走吧，贝尔曼小姐。”
玛格丽特心里反感这口吻——这几乎就是一道命令——可她还是走在两人身边。一出宅子，奥尔加停下脚步，抬手一指。
“您一定得看看那口井。您对古物有兴趣吗？”奥尔加边问边在头前引路，往灌木丛走去。
“我对新东西更感兴趣，尤其是新的阅历，”里德先生颇为快活地说，“新认识的人，更叫我着迷！”
又是她那种倏忽而惊惶的微笑。
“那您该是赶上平生最得意的时光了，里德先生，”她说，“因为您在这儿遇到的人，都是您从前从未遇到过的。”

===Original===
He screwed up his forehead in a frown.
“Yes, there are two people in this house I have never met before,” he said, and she looked round at him quickly.
“Only two? You’ve never met me before!”
“I’ve seen you,” said Mr. Reeder, “but I have never met you.”
By this time they had arrived at the well, and he read the inscription slowly before he tested the board that covered the top of the well with his foot.
“It has been closed for years,” said the girl. “I shouldn’t touch it,” she added hastily, as Reeder stooped and, catching the edge of the board, swung it back trap fashion, leaving an oblong cavity.
The trap did not squeak or creak as he turned it back; the hinges were oiled; there was no accumulation of dust between the two doors. Going on to his hands and knees, he looked down into the darkness.

===Chinese===
他皱起眉头，额头拧作一团。
“不错，这宅子里有两个人物，是我从未遇见过的，”他说。她飞快地回头看了他一眼。
“只有两个？您从前可没遇见过我！”
“我见过您，”里德先生说，“但我还从未结识过您。”
这时他们已到了井边。他先把井上的题铭慢慢读了一遍，才用脚试了试封住井口的那块木板。
“这井封了好多年了，”姑娘说，“别去动它。”她又急忙补了一句——里德先生已经弯下腰去，扣住木板边缘，像翻活板似的把它掀了过去，露出一方长方形的窟窿。
活板翻起时没有一丝吱呀声；铰链上过油；两扇板页之间也没有积尘。他手脚着地趴下去，朝黑暗里张望。

===Original===
“How many loads of rubble and rock were used to fill up this well?” he asked.
Margaret read from the little notice board.
“Hum!” said Mr. Reeder, groped in his pockets, took out a two-shilling piece, poised the silver coin carefully and let it drop.
For a long, long time he listened, and then a faint metallic tinkle came up to him.
“Nine seconds!” He looked up into Olga’s face. “Deduct from the velocity of a falling object the speed at which sound travels, and tell me how deep this hole is.”
He got to his feet, dusted the knees of his trousers, and carefully dropped the trap into position.
“Rock there may be,” he said, “but there is no water. I must work out the number of loads requisite to fill this well entirely﻿—it will be an interesting morning’s occupation for one who in his youth was something of a mathematical genius.”
Olga Crewe led the way back to the shrubbery in silence. When they came to the open: “I think you had better show Mr. Reeder the rest of the establishment,” she said. “I’m rather tired.” And with a nod, she turned away and walked toward the house.

===Chinese===
“填这口井，用去了多少车碎石和石块？”他问。
玛格丽特照着那块小小的告示牌念了出来。
“哼！”里德先生应了一声，在口袋里摸索一气，掏出一枚两先令的硬币，小心地把那枚银币掂稳，松手让它落了下去。
他听了好久好久，才有一声微弱的金属叮响传上他耳朵。
“九秒钟！”他抬眼望着奥尔加的脸，“从落体的速度里扣掉声速，再告诉我这洞有多深。”
他站起身，掸掉裤膝上的土，小心翼翼地把活板放回原位。
“石块嘛，兴许是有的，”他说，“可里头没有水。我得算出把整口井填满所需的车数——对年轻时也算得上一号数学天才的人来说，这倒是桩有趣的晨间消遣。”
奥尔加·克鲁默默领着他们往灌木丛走回去。到了开阔处，她说：“我看这产业剩下的地方，还是您领里德先生去看吧。我有点乏了。”说着点点头，转身朝宅子走去。

===Original===
Mr. Reeder gazed after her with something like admiration in his eyes.
“The rouge would, of course, make a tremendous difference,” he said, half speaking to himself, “but it is very difficult to disguise voices﻿—even the best of actors fail in this respect.”
Margaret stared at him.
“Are you talking to me?”
“To myself,” said Mr. Reeder humbly. “It is a bad habit of mine, peculiar to my age, I fear.”
“But Miss Crewe never uses rouge.”
“Who does﻿—in the country?” asked Mr. Reeder, and pointed with his walking stick to the wall along the cliff. “Where does that lead? What is on the other side?”
“Sudden death,” said Margaret, and laughed.

===Chinese===
里德先生目送着她，眼里近乎含着赞赏。
“胭脂嘛，当然会天差地别，”他半是自言自语地说，“可嗓音是极难伪装的——就连最了不起的演员，也栽在这一样上。”
玛格丽特怔怔地望着他。
“您是在跟我说话吗？”
“跟我自己，”里德先生谦卑地说，“这是我的一个坏习惯，我恐怕，是我们这把年纪的人特有的。”
“可克鲁小姐从来不搽胭脂。”
“谁搽呢——在乡下？”里德先生问，并用手杖指向沿崖而筑的那道墙，“那道墙通向哪里？墙那边是什么？”
“突然死亡。”玛格丽特说着，笑了。

===Original===
For a quarter of an hour they stood leaning on the parapet of the low wall, looking down at the strip of beach below. The small channel that led to the cave interested him. He asked her how deep it was. She thought that it was quite shallow, a conclusion with which he did not agree.
“Underground caves sound romantic, and that channel is deeper than most. I think I must explore the cave. How does one get down?”
He looked left and right. The beach was enclosed in a deep little bay, circled on one side by sheer cliff, on the other by a high reef of rock that ran far out to sea. Mr. Reeder pointed to the horizon.
“Sixty miles from here is France.”
He had a disconcerting habit of going off at a tangent.
“I think I will do a little exploring this afternoon. The walk should freshen me.”

===Chinese===
有一刻钟的工夫，两人倚着矮墙的墙栏站定，俯瞰底下的那一线沙滩。通往岩洞的那条小小水道引起了他的兴趣。他问她那水道有多深。她觉得相当浅——这结论他可不敢苟同。
“地下岩洞听着浪漫，而那条水道比多数的都深。我想我非得探一探那洞不可。从哪儿下去呢？”
他左右张望。这片沙滩围在一处幽深的小海湾里，一侧是壁立的悬崖，另一侧是一道高高的礁石，远远伸进海里。里德先生指向天边。
“离这儿六十英里，就是法国。”
他有个叫人措手不及的习惯：说话动不动就岔到斜刺里去。
“我想今天下午就去小小地探一探。走一走，该能让我精神精神。”

===Original===
They were returning to the house when he remembered the bathing pool and asked to see it.
“I wonder Mr. Daver doesn’t let it run dry,” Margaret said. “It is an awful expense. I was going through the municipality’s account yesterday, and they charge a fabulous sum for pumping up fresh water.”
“How long has it been built?”
“That is the surprising thing,” she said. “It was made twelve years ago, when private swimming pools were things unheard of in this country.”
The pool was oblong in shape; one end of it was tiled and obviously artificially created. The farther end, however, had for its sides and bottom natural rock. A great dome-shaped mass served as a diving platform. Mr. Reeder walked all round, gazing into the limpid water. It was deepest at the rocky end, and here he stayed longest, and his inspection was most thorough. There seemed a space﻿—how deep he could not tell﻿—at the bottom of the bath, where the rock overhung.
“Very interesting,” said Mr. Reeder at last. “I think I will go back to the house and get my bathing suit. Happily I brought one.”

===Chinese===
两人正往回走，他记起了游泳池，便要求去看看。
“我真不明白，达弗先生怎么不干脆由它干涸，”玛格丽特说，“这开销可吓人了。昨天我正在查市政当局的账，把淡水抽上来，他们收的钱高得离谱。”
“这池子修了多少年了？”
“这正是叫人吃惊的地方，”她说，“它是十二年前修的，那时候，私家游泳池在这个国家还是闻所未闻的东西。”
游泳池是长方形的；一端贴着瓷砖，显系人工所筑。可较远的那一端，池壁池底却是天然岩石。一大块穹顶状的岩体权充跳水台。里德先生绕池走了一整圈，凝望着澄澈的池水。岩石那一端最深，他在此盘桓最久，察看也最细。池底岩壁悬出的地方，似乎有一处空当——多深，他说不上来。
“非常有意思，”里德先生终于开口，“我想我这就回宅子去取游泳衣。幸亏我带了一件。”

===Original===
“I didn’t know you were a swimmer,” smiled the girl.
“I am the merest tyro in most things,” said Mr. Reeder modestly.
He went up to his room, undressed and slipped into a bathing suit, over which he put his overcoat. Olga Crewe and Mr. Daver had gone down to Siltbury. To his satisfaction, he saw the hotel car descending the hill road cautiously in a cloud of dust.
When Mr. Reeder threw off his coat to make the plunge there was something comically ferocious in his appearance, for about his waist he had fastened a belt to which was fastened in a sheath a long-bladed hunting knife, and in addition there dangled a waterproof bag in which he had placed one of the many little hand lamps that he invariably carried about with him. He made the most human preparations: put his toes into the cold water, and shivered ecstatically before he made his plunge. Losing no time in preliminaries, he swam along the bottom to the slit in the rock which he had seen.
It was about two feet high and eight feet in length, and into this he pulled his way, gripping the roof to aid his progress. The roof ended abruptly; he found nothing but water above him, and he allowed himself to come to the surface, catching hold of a projecting ledge to keep himself afloat whilst he detached the waterproof bag from his belt, and, planting it upon the shelf, took out his flashlamp.
He was in a natural stone chamber, with a broad, vaulted roof. He was, in fact, inside the dome-shaped rock that formed one end of the pool. At the farthermost corner of the chamber was an opening about four feet in height and two feet in width. A rock passage that led downward, he saw. He followed this for about fifty yards and noted that, although nature had hewn or worn this queer corridor at some remote age﻿—possibly it had been an underground waterway before some gigantic upheaval of nature had raised the land above water level﻿—the passage owed something of its practicability to human agency. At one place there were marks of a chisel; at another, unmistakable signs of blasting. Mr. Reeder retraced his steps and came back to the water. He fastened and resealed his lamp, and, drawing a long breath, dived to the bottom and wormed his way through the aperture to the bath and to open air. He came to the surface to gaze into the horror-stricken face of Margaret Belman.

===Chinese===
“我还不知道您会游泳呢。”姑娘微笑道。
“在大多数事情上，我都是个再纯粹不过的生手，”里德先生谦逊地说。
他上楼回到房间，脱了衣裳，换上游泳衣，外面罩了大衣。奥尔加·克鲁和达弗先生已经下山去西尔特伯里（Siltbury）了。他满意地看到，旅馆的汽车正扬着一溜尘土，小心翼翼地沿山道往下驶去。
里德先生甩掉大衣准备下水时，那模样颇有几分滑稽的凶悍：他腰上系了一条皮带，鞘里别着一柄长刃猎刀，此外还晃荡着一只防水袋，里头装着他随身必带的许多小手灯中的一盏。他做的预备功夫最有人味儿：先把脚尖探进冷水，美滋滋地打了个寒噤，这才纵身入水。预备动作一概从简，他贴着池底，直向他看见的那道岩缝游去。
那岩缝约两英尺高、八英尺长，他攀着顶壁借力，一路钻了进去。顶壁陡然到了头；他发觉头顶上只剩一片水，便任自己浮上水面，抓住一处凸出的石台稳住身子，一面从腰带上解下防水袋，搁上台子，取出手电。
他置身于一间天然石室之中，顶上是宽阔的穹隆。实际上，他正在那块构成泳池一端的穹顶状岩石里面。石室最远的角落里有一道口子，约四英尺高、两英尺宽。他看出那是一条向下延伸的岩石通道。他循着走了约五十码，并注意到：这条古怪的走廊虽是自然界在某个遥远年代凿出或磨蚀而成——或许在某次天翻地覆的大变动把这片陆地抬出水面之前，它原是一条地下水道——但通道之所以通行无碍，多少有人力的功劳。一处留有凿子的痕迹；另一处则有爆破的明证，错认不了。里德先生循原路折回水边。他扣好手灯，重新封严，深吸一口气，潜到池底，从那道口子里蜿蜒钻出，回到泳池，回到露天的空气里。他浮出水面，正对上玛格丽特·贝尔曼那张惊骇失色的脸。

===Original===
“Oh, Mr. Reeder!” she gasped. “You﻿—you frightened me! I heard you jump in, but when I came here and found the bath empty I thought I must have been mistaken. Where have you been? You couldn’t stay under water all that time.”
“Will you hand me my overcoat?” said Mr. Reeder modestly, and when he had hastily buttoned this about his person: “I have been to see that the County Council’s requirements are fully satisfied,” he said solemnly.
She listened, dazed.
“In all theatres, as you probably know, my dear Miss﻿—um﻿—Margaret, it is essential that there should be certain exits in case of necessity. I have already inspected two this morning, but I rather imagine that the most important of all has so far escaped my observation. What a man! Surely madness is akin to genius!”

===Chinese===
“哎呀，里德先生！”她倒抽一口气，“您——您吓死我了！我听见您跳下水，可等我过来一看，池子里空空的，我还以为是自己听岔了。您上哪儿去了？那么长的工夫，谁也不可能闷在水底下呀。”
“麻烦您把我的大衣递过来？”里德先生谦逊地说。等他把大衣往身上匆匆一裹、扣好扣子，又说：“我是去查看郡议会的各项要求是否都得到了充分满足。”他说得一本正经。
她听得发懵。
“您多半知道，亲爱的——嗯——玛格丽特小姐，凡属戏院，都必须备有若干太平门，以防万一。今天早上我已经查验过两座了，不过我总觉得，最要紧的那一座，至今还漏在我的观察之外。了不起的人物！疯狂想必与天才是近亲！”

===Original===
---
He lunched alone, and apparently no man was less interested in his fellow guests than Mr. J. G. Reeder. The two golfers had returned and were eating at the same table. Miss Crewe, who came in late and favoured him with a smile, sat at a little table facing him.
“She is uneasy,” said Mr. Reeder to himself. “That is the second time she has dropped her fork. Presently she will get up, sit with her back to me﻿—I wonder on what excuse?”
Apparently no excuse was necessary. The girl called a waitress toward her and had her glass and tableware shifted to the other side of the table. Mr. Reeder was rather pleased with himself.
Daver minced into the dining room as Mr. Reeder was peeling an apple.
“Good morning, Mr. Reeder. Have you got over your nightmare? I see that you have! A man of iron nerve. I admire that tremendously. Personally, I am the most dreadful coward, and the very hint of a burglar makes me shiver. You wouldn’t believe it, but I had a quarrel with a servant this morning and she left me shaking! You are not affected that way? I see that you are not! Miss Belman tells me that you tried our swimming pool this morning. You enjoyed it? I am sure you did!”
“Won’t you sit down and have coffee?” asked Mr. Reeder politely, but Daver declined the invitation with a flourish and a bow.
“No, no, I have my work. I cannot tell you how grateful I am to Miss Belman for putting me on the track of the most fascinating character of modern times. What a man!” said Mr. Daver, unconsciously repeating J. G. Reeder’s tribute. “I’ve been trying to trace his early career﻿—no, no, I’ll stand: I must run away in a minute or two. Is anything known about his early life? Was he married?”

===Chinese===
---
他独自用了午餐，而表面上，再没有谁比 J. G. 里德更不把同宿的客人放在心上了。那两位打高尔夫的先生已经回来，正在同一张桌上用餐。克鲁小姐来得迟，冲他惠然一笑，在正对着他的一张小桌旁坐下。
“她心神不定，”里德先生心里说，“这是她第二回掉叉子了。过一会儿她就要起身，换到背朝我坐——不知要找个什么借口？”
显然连借口都无须找。她招手唤来一名女侍，叫人把杯盏刀叉挪到了桌子另一侧。里德先生对自己相当满意。
里德先生正削着苹果，达弗扭扭捏捏地踱进饭厅。
“早安，里德先生。您的噩梦缓过来了吗？我看缓过来了！真是条铁打的神经。这一点我佩服得五体投地。至于我自己，我是个顶顶厉害的胆小鬼，只要隐隐提一声贼，我就要发抖。您准不信，今天早上我跟一个仆人吵了一架，她走的时候我还在抖呢！您不会这样吧？我看您不会！贝尔曼小姐告诉我，您今天早上试了我们的游泳池。游得痛快吧？我敢保痛快！”
“您不坐下喝杯咖啡吗？”里德先生客气地问。可达弗把手一挥、身子一躬，谢绝了这番好意。
“不坐不坐，我还有我的工作。贝尔曼小姐替我指点了我最感激的人——当代最引人入胜的人物的行踪，我对她的感激，实在无以言表。了不起的人物！”达弗先生说着，不自觉地重复了 J. G. 里德的赞语，“我一直在追索他早年的经历——不不，我站着就行：过一两分钟我就得跑了。他的早年生涯有什么可考的吗？他结过婚吗？”

===Original===
Mr. Reeder nodded. He had not the slightest idea that John Flack was married, but it seemed a moment to assert the universality of his knowledge. He was quite unprepared for the effect upon Daver. The jaw of the yellow-faced man dropped.
“Married?” he squeaked. “Who told you he was married? Where was he married?”
“That is a matter,” said Mr. Reeder gravely, “which I cannot discuss.”
“Married!” Daver rubbed his little round head irritably, but did not pursue the subject. He made some inane reference to the weather and bustled out of the room.
Mr. Reeder settled himself in what he called the banqueting hall with an illustrated paper, awaiting an opportunity which he knew must present itself sooner or later.
The servants he had passed under review. Girls were employed to wait at table, and these lived in a small cottage on the Siltbury side of the estate. The manservants, including the hall porter, seemed above suspicion. The porter was an old army man with a row of medals across his uniform jacket; his assistant was a chinless youth recruited from Siltbury. He apparently was the only member of the staff that did not live in one of the cottages. In the main, the women servants were an unpromising lot. The infuriated waitress was his only hope, although as likely as not she would talk of nothing but her grievances.
From where he sat he had a view of the lawn. At three o’clock the Colonel and the Rev. Mr. Dean and Olga Crewe passed out of the main gate, evidently bound for Siltbury. He rang the bell and, to his satisfaction, the aggrieved waitress came and took his order for tea.

===Chinese===
里德先生点了点头。约翰·弗拉克结没结过婚，他连一丁点儿底也没有，可这一刻，似乎正宜摆一摆他无所不知的谱。达弗闻言的反应，他却全然不曾料到。那张黄脸汉子的下巴耷拉了下来。
“结过婚？”他尖叫起来，“谁告诉您他结过婚的？在哪儿结的？”
“此事，”里德先生正色道，“无可奉告。”
“结过婚！”达弗烦躁地搓着他那颗小圆脑袋，却没有再追这个话头。他就天气胡扯了几句，风风火火地出了饭厅。
里德先生揣着一份画报，在他所谓的宴会厅里安顿下来，静候一个他知道迟早必会出现的机会。
仆人们，他已逐一掂量过了。餐桌旁用的是姑娘们，她们住在产业靠西尔特伯里一侧的一座小下房里。男仆们，包括门房在内，似乎都无可怀疑。门房是个行伍出身的老人，制服前襟上横着一排勋章；他的助手是个没有下巴的小伙子，从西尔特伯里招来的。他显然是员工中唯一不住在下房里的一个。大体而言，女仆们是叫人不敢指望的一群。那个怒气冲冲的女侍是他唯一的指望，虽然十有八九，她开口闭口只会数落自己的委屈。
从他坐的地方望得见草坪。三点钟，上校、迪恩牧师和奥尔加·克鲁出了大门，显然是往西尔特伯里去。他按了铃，令他满意的是，来的正是那位满腹委屈的女侍，听他点了茶。

===Original===
“This is a nice place,” said Mr. Reeder conversationally.
The girl’s “Yes, sir” was snappy.
“I suppose,” mused Mr. Reeder, looking out of the window, “that this is the sort of situation that a lot of girls would give their heads to get and break their hearts to lose?”
Evidently she did not agree.
“The upstairs work isn’t so bad,” she said, “and there’s not much to do in the dining room. But it’s too slow for me. I was at a big hotel before I came here. I’m going to a better job﻿—and the sooner the better.”
She admitted that the money was good, but she had a longing for that imponderable quantity which she described as “life.” She also expressed a preference for man guests.
“Miss Crewe﻿—so called﻿—gives more trouble than all the rest of the people put together,” she said. “I can’t make her out. First she wants one room, then she wants another. Why she can’t stay with her husband, I don’t know.”

===Chinese===
“这地方不错，”里德先生搭讪着说。
姑娘那声“是的，先生”又快又冲。
“我想，”里德先生望着窗外出神地说，“这样的位子，多少姑娘挤破了头也想谋着，丢了又要哭断肠吧？”
她显然不同意。
“楼上的活计不算累，”她说，“饭厅里也没多少事做。可对我来说太闷了。来这儿之前，我在一家大旅馆干。我要去更好的差事——越早越好。”
她承认工钱不坏，可她渴慕着一样无从称量的东西，她管它叫“生活”。她还表示，自己更待见男客人。
“克鲁小姐——姑且这么叫吧——招的麻烦，比其余所有人加在一起还多，”她说，“我摸不透她。一会儿要这间房，一会儿要那间房。她干吗不能跟她丈夫一块儿住，我真不明白。”

===Original===
“With her﻿—?” Mr. Reeder looked at her in pained surprise. “Perhaps they don’t get on well together?”
“They used to get on all right. If they weren’t married, I could understand all the mystery they’re making﻿—pretending they’re not, him in his room and she in hers, and meeting like strangers. When all that kind of deceit is going on, things are bound to get lost,” she added inconsequently.
“How long has this been﻿—er﻿—going on?” asked Mr. Reeder.
“Only the last week or so,” said the girl viciously. “I know they’re married, because I’ve seen her marriage certificate﻿—they’ve been married six years. She keeps it in her dressing case.”
She looked at him with sudden suspicion.
“I oughtn’t to have told you that. I don’t want to make trouble for anybody, and I bear them no malice, though they’ve treated me worse’n a dog,” she said. “Nobody else in the house but me knows. I was her maid for two years. But if people don’t treat me right, I don’t treat them right.”
“Married six years? Dear me!” said Mr. Reeder.

===Chinese===
“跟她——？”里德先生一脸痛心惊讶地望着她，“莫非他们处得不融洽？”
“他们从前处得好好的。要是他们没结婚，这其中的蹊跷我倒能明白——装作不是两口子，他住他的房，她住她的，见了面跟生人似的。这种种欺瞒的勾当一多，东西保准就要丢。”她又没头没尾地补了一句。
“这种情形——呃——有多久了？”里德先生问。
“不过最近一个来星期，”姑娘狠狠地说，“我知道他们是两口子，因为我见过她的结婚证书——他们结婚六年了。她把证书收在梳妆盒里。”
她忽然起了疑心，看了他一眼。
“这话我本不该告诉您。我不想给谁惹麻烦，我对他们也没有恶意，虽说他们待我还不如待条狗，”她说，“这宅子里除了我，没别人知道。我给她当过两年贴身女仆。可要是人家不好好待我，我也不会好好待人家。”
“结婚六年了？天哪！”里德先生说。

===Original===
And then he suddenly turned his head and faced her.
“Would you like fifty pounds?” he asked. “That is the immense sum I will give you for just one little peep at that marriage certificate.”
The girl went red.
“You’re trying to catch me,” she said, hesitated, and then: “I don’t want to get her into trouble.”
“I am a detective,” said Mr. Reeder, “but I am working on behalf of the Chief Registrar, and we have a doubt as to whether that marriage was legal. I could, of course, search the young lady’s room and find the certificate for myself, but if you would care to help me, and fifty pounds has any attraction for you﻿—”
She paused irresolutely and said she would see. Half an hour later she came into the hall with the news that she had been unsuccessful in her search. She had found the envelope in which the certificate had been kept, but the document itself was gone.
Mr. Reeder did not ask the name of the bridegroom, nor was he mentioned, for he was pretty certain that he knew that fortunate man. He put the question, and the girl answered as he had expected.

===Chinese===
接着他忽地扭过头来，正对着她。
“您想不想要五十镑？”他问，“只要让我对那张结婚证书小小地瞟上一眼，这笔巨款就归您。”
姑娘的脸红了。
“您这是设套儿逮我，”她说，迟疑了一下，又道，“我不想害她惹上麻烦。”
“我是侦探不假，”里德先生说，“可我这回是替首席注册官办事，我们对那桩婚事合不合法存着疑问。当然，我可以搜那位年轻小姐的房间，自己把证书找出来；不过，倘使您愿意帮把手，而五十镑对您又有几分吸引力——”
她迟疑不决地顿了顿，说要试试看。半小时后她进到厅里来报信：找是没找到。她寻见了原先装证书的那只信封，可证书本身已经不翼而飞。
里德先生没有问新郎姓甚名谁，对方也没提起，因为那位幸运儿是谁，他已十拿九稳。他提了另一个问题，姑娘的回答正如他所料。

===Original===
“There is one thing I would like to ask you: do you remember the name of the girl’s father?”
“John Crewe, merchant,” she said promptly. “The mother’s name was Hannah. He made me swear on the Bible I’d never tell a soul that I knew they were married.”
“Does anybody else know? You said ‘nobody,’ I think?”
The girl hesitated.
“Yes, Mrs. Burton knows. She knows everything.”
“Thank you,” said Mr. Reeder, and, opening his pocketbook, took out two five-pound notes. “What was the husband’s profession﻿—do you remember that?”
The woman’s lips curled.
“Secretary. Why call himself secretary, I don’t know, and him an independent gentleman!”

===Chinese===
“有件事我想问问您：那姑娘父亲的名字，您还记得吗？”
“约翰·克鲁，商人，”她应声答道，“母亲叫汉娜。他逼我按着《圣经》起誓，绝不向任何人吐露我知道他们结了婚。”
“还有别人知道吗？我记得您说过‘没有别人’？”
姑娘迟疑了。
“有，伯顿太太（Mrs. Burton）知道。她什么都知道。”
“谢谢，”里德先生说着打开皮夹子，抽出两张五镑的钞票，“那位丈夫是干什么的——这个您还记得吗？”
女人的嘴角撇了撇。
“秘书。他干吗要自称秘书，我真闹不懂——他可是位不用仰人鼻息的绅士！”

===Original===
“Thank you,” said Mr. Reeder again.
He telephoned to Siltbury for a taxicab.
“Are you going out?” asked Margaret, finding him waiting under the portico.
“I am buying a few presents for friends in London,” said Mr. Reeder glibly; “a butter dish or two, suitably inscribed, would, I feel sure, be very acceptable.”
The taxi did not take him to Siltbury. Instead, he followed a road which ran parallel with the sea coast, and which eventually landed him in an impossible sandy track, from which the ancient taxi was extricated with some difficulty.
“I told you this led nowhere, sir,” said the aggrieved driver.
“Then we have evidently reached our destination,” replied Mr. Reeder, applying his weight to push the machine to a more solid foundation.

===Chinese===
“谢谢。”里德先生又说了一遍。
他打电话到西尔特伯里，叫了一辆出租汽车。
“您要出门？”玛格丽特看见他等在门廊底下，问道。
“我去给伦敦的朋友们买些小礼物，”里德先生说得上溜极了，“一两只黄油碟，再题上合适的字，我相信，一定非常受欢迎。”
出租汽车并没有载他去西尔特伯里。它走的是一条与海岸平行的路，末了把他撂进一条没法走的沙道里，那辆老客车费了好一番周折才挣脱出来。
“我早跟您说了，这条路哪儿也到不了，先生。”那位一肚子委屈的司机说。
“那么我们显然已经到了目的地。”里德先生答道，一边把全身的分量压上去，把车推到较硬实的地面上。

===Original===
Siltbury was not greatly favoured by London visitors, the driver told him on the way back. The town had a pebbly beach and people preferred sand.
“There are some wonderful beaches about here,” said the driver, “but you can’t reach ’em.”
They had taken the left-hand road, which would bring them eventually to the town, and had been driving for a quarter of an hour when Mr. Reeder, who sat by the driver, pointed to a large scar in the face of the downs on his right.
“Siltbury Quarries,” explained the cabman. “They’re not worked now; there are too many holes.”
“Holes?”
“The downs are like a sponge,” said the man. “You could lose yourself in the caves. Old Mr. Kimpon used to work the quarries many years ago, and it broke him. There’s a big cave there you can drive a coach and four into! About twenty years ago, three fellows went in to explore the caves and never came out again.”
“Who owns the quarry now?”

===Chinese===
归途上司机告诉他，西尔特伯里并不怎么受伦敦游客的青睐。这镇子有的是卵石滩，而人们偏爱沙子。
“这一带有些海滩妙极了，”司机说，“可您到不了。”
他们走的是左边那条路，到头可以回镇上去。车行了约一刻钟，坐在司机身旁的里德先生指向右手边丘陵坡面上的一道大疤。
“西尔特伯里采石场，”车夫解释道，“现在不采了；窟窿太多。”
“窟窿？”
“这片丘陵就跟块海绵似的，”那人说，“进了那些洞，您能把自己走丢了。老金彭先生（Kimpon）好多年前经营这采石场，就败在它手里。那儿有个大洞，赶着一辆四驾大马车都能进去！约摸二十年前，有三个家伙进洞探险，再没出来。”
“这采石场现在归谁？”

===Original===
Mr. Reeder wasn’t very interested, but when his mind was occupied with a pressing problem he had a trick of flogging along a conversation with appropriate questions, and if he was oblivious of the answers they produced, the sound of the human voice had a sedative effect.
“Mr. Daver owns it now. He bought it after the people were lost in the caves, and had the entrance boarded up. You’ll see it in a minute.”
They were climbing a gentle slope. As they came to the crest, he pointed down a tidy-looking roadway to where, about two hundred yards distant, Reeder saw an oblong gap in the white face of the quarry. Across this, and falling the cavity except for an irregular space at the top, was a heavy wooden gate.
“You can’t see it from here,” said the driver, “but the top hole is blocked with barbed wire.”
“Is that a gate or a hoarding he has fixed across?”
“A gate, sir. Mr. Daver owns all the land from here to the sea. He used to farm about a hundred acres of the downs, but it’s very poor land. In those days he kept his wagons inside the cave.”
“When did he give up farming?” asked Mr. Reeder, interested.
“About six years ago,” was the reply, and it was exactly the reply Mr. Reeder had expected. “I used to see a lot of Mr. Daver before then,” said the driver. “In the old times I had a horse cab, and I was always driving him about. He used to work like a slave﻿—on the farm in the morning, down in the town buying things in the afternoon. He was more like a servant than a master. He used to meet all the trains when visitors arrived﻿—and they had a lot of visitors in those days, more than they have now. Sometimes he went up to London to bring them down. He always went to meet Miss Crewe when the young lady was at school.”

===Chinese===
里德先生并不怎么感兴趣，可当脑子里盘着一桩紧要问题时，他有个法子：用得体的问题抽着话头往前赶；这些问话引出的回答，他纵然一概充耳不闻，人声本身却有种安神的功效。
“现在归达弗先生。那些人困死在洞里之后，他把场子买了下来，还叫人用木板把洞口封了。一会儿您就看见了。”
车子正爬一道缓坡。到了坡顶，司机顺着一条齐整的行车道指过去——约两百码开外，里德看见采石场白色的坡面上豁着一道长方形的口子。横堵在口上、几乎填满整个洞腔、只顶端留出一道不规则空隙的，是一扇厚重的木门。
“打这儿瞧不见，”司机说，“顶上那个窟窿，拿铁丝网封着呢。”
“他横在那儿的是扇门呢，还是堵栅栏？”
“是扇门，先生。从这儿到海边，地全是达弗先生的。他从前在丘陵上种着约一百英亩地，可那地太贫了。那些年月，他的运货大车就存放在山洞里。”
“他什么时候不种地了？”里德先生问，来了兴致。
“约摸六年前吧。”回答来得干脆，而这正是里德先生料想分毫不差的回答。“在那之前，我常常见到达弗先生，”司机说，“早年间我赶的是马车，老载着他到处跑。他从前干活跟个苦力似的——早上在农场，下午下镇买东西。他与其说像个主人，不如说像个仆人。客人到的时候，趟趟火车他都去接——那些年他们客人多，比现在多。有时他还上伦敦去接。那位克鲁小姐上学的时候，回回都是他去接。”

===Original===
“Do you know Miss Crewe?”
Apparently the driver had seen her frequently, but his acquaintance was very limited.
Reeder got down from the cab and climbed the barred gate on to the private roadway. The soil was chalky and the road had the appearance of having been recently overhauled. He mentioned this fact to the cabman and learned that Mr. Daver kept two old men constantly at work making up the road, though why he should do so he had no idea.
“Where would you like to go now, sir?”
“To a quiet place where I can telephone,” said Mr. Reeder.
These were the facts that he carried with him, and vital facts they were. During the past six years, the life of Mr. Daver had undergone a considerable change. From being a harassed man of affairs, “more like a servant than a master,” he had become a gentleman of leisure. The mystery of the Keep was a mystery no longer. He got Inspector Simpson on the telephone and conveyed to him the gist of his discovery.
“By the way,” said Simpson at the finish, “the gold hasn’t been sent to Australia yet. There has been trouble at the docks. You don’t seriously anticipate a Flack ‘operation,’ do you?”
Mr. Reeder, who had forgotten all about the gold convoy, made a cautious and noncommittal reply.

===Chinese===
“您认识克鲁小姐？”
看样子司机常常见到她，可交情实在有限。
里德下了车，攀过那扇上着栅条的大门，踏上私家行道。土是白垩土，路面一副新近翻修过的模样。他向车夫提了这一句，得知达弗先生常年雇着两个老人修路——为的是什么，车夫就说不上了。
“先生，您这会儿想上哪儿？”
“找个能打电话的清静地方。”里德先生说。
他随身带走的，便是这些事实，而且是攸关紧要的事实。过去这六年里，达弗先生的生活经历了不小的变化。从一个操劳奔命的生意人，一个“与其说像主人、不如说像仆人”的人，他变成了一位有闲绅士。主堡之谜，从此不再是谜。他接通了辛普森督察（Inspector Simpson）的电话，把自己此番发现的要点讲给了他。
“对了，”末了辛普森说，“那批黄金还没发往澳大利亚。码头上出了乱子。您总不至于当真料到弗拉克要来一手‘动作’吧？”
里德先生早已把黄金押运的事忘了个干净，只得谨慎而不置可否地应了几句。

===Original===
By the time he returned to Larmes Keep, the other guests had returned. The hall porter said they were expecting a “party” on the morrow, but as he had volunteered that information on the previous evening Mr. Reeder did not take it very seriously. He gathered that the man spoke in good faith, without any wish to deceive, but he saw no signs of unusual activity; nor, indeed, was there accommodation at the Keep for more than a few more visitors.
He looked around for the aggrieved servant and missed her. A discreet inquiry revealed the fact that she had left that afternoon.
Mr. Reeder went to his room, locked the door, and busied himself in the examination of two great scrapbooks which he had brought down with him. They were the official records of Flack and his gang. Perhaps “gang” was hardly a proper description, for he seemed to use and change his associates as a theatrical manager uses and changes his cast. The police knew close on a score of men who from time to time had assisted John Flack in his nefarious transactions. Some had gone to prison, and had spent the hours of their recovered liberty in a vain endeavour to reestablish touch with so generous a paymaster. Some, known to be in his employ, had vanished, and were generally supposed to be living in luxury abroad.
Reeder went through the book, which was full of essential facts, and jotted down the amounts which this strange man had acquired in the course of twenty years’ depredations. The total was a staggering one. Flack had worked feverishly, and though he had paid well he had spent little. Somewhere in England was an enormous reserve. And that somewhere, Mr. Reeder guessed, was very close to his hand.
For what had John Flack worked? To what end was this accumulation of money? Was the sheer greed of the miser behind his thefts? Was he working aimlessly, as a madman works, toward some visionary objective?
Flack’s greed was proverbial. Nothing satisfied him. The robbery of the Leadenhall Bank had been followed a week later by an attack upon the London Trust Syndicate, carried out, the police discovered, by an entirely new confederation, gathered within a few days of the robbery and yet so perfectly rehearsed that the plan was carried through without a hitch.

===Chinese===
待他回到拉尔姆宅，别的客人也都回来了。门房说，明天要来一拨“客人”，不过这话头一晚他已经主动交代过，里德先生便没怎么往心里去。他听得出来，这人说话出于好意，并无欺瞒之心；可他看不出有什么非常忙碌的迹象；何况主堡的客房，也实在只容得下再添寥寥数位客人。
他四下寻那位满腹委屈的女仆，却不见了人。一番不打眼的打听探明：她当天下午已经走了。
里德先生回到自己房里，锁上门，埋头翻检起随身带来的两大本剪贴簿。那是弗拉克及其团伙的官方档案。也许“团伙”一词并不十分贴切，因为他使用、更换手下，就像一位剧院经理使用、更换他的班底。警方前后认得将近二十个曾不时协助约翰·弗拉克干那等罪恶买卖的人。有的进了监狱，重获自由后又把光阴尽数耗在徒劳的钻营上，想同这样一位出手豪阔的东家续上旧谊。有的明知受雇于他，却已人间蒸发，一般都认定是在国外享着清福。
里德把簿子从头到尾过了一遍——里头尽是紧要的事实——并把这个怪人二十年间劫掠所得的各项数目一一摘记下来。总数骇人听闻。弗拉克干得如痴如狂，虽说出手豪阔，自家却花得极少。英格兰的某个地方，藏着一大笔储备金。而那个地方，里德先生猜，就近在咫尺。
约翰·弗拉克图的是什么？这堆积如山的钱财，为的是个什么结局？他的盗窃背后，是守财奴那种纯粹的贪欲吗？还是他正像一个疯子那样漫无目的地劳作，朝着某个虚幻的目标？
弗拉克的贪心是出了名的。什么也填不满他。莱登霍尔银行劫案过后不过一星期，伦敦信托银团（London Trust Syndicate）又遭了殃——警方发现，动手的是一个全新的团伙，劫案后没几天才凑齐，可排演得又那样到家，整个计划执行得天衣无缝。

===Original===
Mr. Reeder locked away his books and went downstairs in search of Margaret Belman. The crisis was very near at hand, and it was necessary for his peace of mind that the girl should leave Larmes Keep without delay.
He was halfway down the stairs when he met Daver coming up, and at that moment he received an inspiration.
“You are the very gentleman I wished to meet,” he said, “I wonder if you would do me a great favour?”
Daver’s careworn face wreathed in smiles.
“My dear Mr. Reeder,” he said enthusiastically, “do you a favour? Command me!”
“I have been thinking about last night and my extraordinary experience,” said Mr. Reeder.
“You mean the burglar?” interrupted the other quickly.

===Chinese===
里德先生把簿子锁好，下楼来找玛格丽特·贝尔曼。危机已迫在眉睫，为求心中安宁，这姑娘必须马上离开拉尔姆宅。
他下到楼梯半腰，迎面遇上正上楼的达弗，就在这一刻，他灵光一现。
“您正是我一心想遇见的绅士，”他说，“不知您肯不肯帮我一个大忙？”
达弗那张饱经忧患的脸顿时堆满了笑。
“亲爱的里德先生，”他热忱地说，“帮我忙？尽管吩咐！”
“我一直在想昨夜的事，想我那番离奇的遭遇，”里德先生说。
“您是说那个贼？”对方赶紧插话。

===Original===
“The burglar,” agreed Mr. Reeder. “He was an alarming person, and I am not disposed to let the matter rest where it is. Fortunately for me, I have found a fingerprint on the panel of my door.”
He saw Daver’s face change.
“When I say I have found a fingerprint, I have found something which has the appearance of a fingerprint, and I can only be sure if I examine it by means of a dactyloscope. Unfortunately, I did not imagine that I should have need for such an instrument, and I am wondering if you could send somebody to London to bring it down for me?”
“With all the pleasure in life,” said Daver, though his tone lacked heartiness. “One of the men﻿—”
